    - This module consumes Module 3's Kerberos, NTLM, LDAP, AD DS, credential validation, service account, ticketing, and hybrid identity foundations by applying them to AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, LSASS credential dumping, cached credential extraction, SAM extraction, Kerberos ticket material, token theft, service account secrets, and managed service account exposure.
- **Module 10 / Chapter 10**
  - **Identity Authority Expansion**
    - This module consumes Module 3's authorization, delegation, ACL, certificate services, trust, and hybrid identity foundations by applying them to ACL / DACL / ACE abuse, delegation abuse, Resource-Based Constrained Delegation (RBCD), Shadow Credentials, AD CS template abuse, PetitPotam, coercion-assisted escalation, relay chains, and Microsoft Entra administrative role abuse.
- **Module 11 / Chapter 11**
  - **Enterprise Identity Mobility**
    - This module consumes Module 3's trust, authentication protocol, remote administration, SMB, RPC, Kerberos, NTLM, and cross-boundary authentication foundations by applying them to Pass-the-Hash, Pass-the-Ticket, Overpass-the-Hash, NTLM Relay, PrinterBug / MS-RPRN, SMB, PsExec, WinRM, WMI, DCOM, RDP, PowerShell Remoting, remote service creation, cross-domain trust traversal, and cross-forest authentication.
- **Module 12 / Chapter 12**
  - **Persistence and Domain Dominance**

- **Module 13 / Chapter 13**
  - **Detection Security Engineering**
    - This module consumes Module 3's infrastructure architecture and translates it into detection engineering for Kerberos anomalies, NTLM abuse, LDAP reconnaissance, DNS anomalies, AD CS abuse, privilege escalation, lateral movement, replication abuse, Group Policy manipulation, federation abuse, identity analytics, AI-assisted detection workflows, alert enrichment, and attack path disruption.
- **Module 14 / Chapter 14**
  - **Identity Recovery and Domain Trust Restoration**
    - This module consumes Module 3's technical understanding of forests, domains, Domain Controllers, replication, Kerberos, KRBTGT, PKI, AD CS, trusts, Group Policy, and hybrid identity to support forest recovery, AD CS recovery, certificate revocation, trust repair, credential rotation, Tier 0 reconstitution, and restoration of confidence in enterprise identity infrastructure.
- **Module 15 / Chapter 15**
  - **Operational Lessons Learned from Federal Identity Operations**
    - This module consumes Module 3's infrastructure foundation by examining real-world failures involving incorrectly designed forests, forgotten trusts, DNS failures, replication problems, PKI drift, unmanaged certificate services, Group Policy sprawl, service account exposure, contractor turnover, and identity infrastructure documentation gaps.
- **Module 16 / Chapter 16**
  - **The Future of Identity-Centric Warfare**
    - This module consumes Module 3's enterprise identity architecture foundation and projects it forward into cloud-native identity. Microsoft Entra ID, passwordless authentication, FIDO2, identity threat detection and response (ITDR), AI-assisted assisted attacks, AI-assisted defense, adversarial AI, post-Kerberos authentication models, Zero Trust modernization, and future federal identity architectures.

Module 3 is the technical architecture dependency for the curriculum because every later module examines identity infrastructure either as an assessment target, engineering baseline, reconnaissance surface, enumeration source, authentication fabric, credential ecosystem, privilege boundary, movement pathway, persistence substrate, detection domain, recovery object, or future modernization platform.

**Offensive Ownership**

This module does not own operational attack execution or specific offensive procedures.

It owns the technical infrastructure premise that adversaries target identity systems because enterprise identity infrastructure concentrates authentication, authorization, credentials, trust relationships, policy enforcement, certificates, federation, replication, and administrative control. This module teaches the architectural foundations that later offensive modules apply when explaining why attacks such as AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, NTLM Relay, AD CS abuse, DCSync, Golden Ticket abuse, and trust traversal are possible.

Specific offensive techniques, tooling, exploitation workflows, and tradecraft are intentionally deferred to later modules.

**Defensive Ownership**

This module owns the defensive infrastructure foundation for identity security. It establishes that effective defense requires understanding how identity services function before attempting to secure, monitor, assess, or recover them.

This module frames defensive identity security as a technical architecture discipline. Readers learn that Domain Controllers, DNS, Kerberos, LDAP, PKI, AD CS, AD FS, Group Policy, replication, trusts, Microsoft Entra ID, and hybrid identity synchronization are not isolated services; they are interdependent control points whose failure, misconfiguration, or compromise can affect enterprise authentication, authorization, privileged administration, mission access, and operational continuity.

Detailed hardening, secure baseline validation, detection engineering, and recovery procedures are deferred to later modules.

**FICAM Ownership**

**Module 3 is the primary instructional home for FICAM.**

Module 3 owns:

- The technical infrastructure that supports FICAM implementation
- Enterprise directory services as identity infrastructure
- Authentication services as identity enforcement mechanisms
- Authorization infrastructure as access enforcement points
- Credential validation through directory, certificate, and authentication services
- Federation services as technical trust infrastructure
- PKI and certificate services as credential and trust infrastructure
- Hybrid identity infrastructure supporting federal identity modernization
- Active Directory as a technical platform for implementing identity, credential, and access management functions
- Microsoft Entra ID as a cloud identity platform supporting modern identity operations
- The governance-to-technology bridge between Module 2 and later operational models

**Module 3 does not own:**

- Foundational FICAM governance
- Detailed PIV/CAC lifecycle governance
- Detailed NIST assurance-level analysis
- FICAM program management
- Security assessment methodology
- Baseline hardening implementation
- Offensive attack execution
- Detection security engineering
- Incident response or recovery

Those concepts are owned or extended in later modules.

Later modules may apply, extend, or reference the identity infrastructure introduced here, but they do not reteach its foundational architecture.

**Zero Trust Ownership**

This module applies Zero Trust at the infrastructure architecture level.

**Module 3 owns:  
**

- The technical identity infrastructure upon which Zero Trust depends
- The role of identity services in verifying users, devices, services, and workloads
- The role of authentication protocols in supporting continuous verification
- The role of authorization infrastructure in enforcing the principle of least privilege
- The role of PKI and certificates in supporting strong identity assertions
- The role of federation services in extending trust across boundaries
- The role of Microsoft Entra ID and hybrid identity in modern Zero Trust architectures
- The relationship between legacy Active Directory environments and Zero Trust modernization

**Module 3 does not own:  
**

- Full Zero Trust Architecture implementation
- DoD Zero Trust Reference Architecture implementation details
- CISA Zero Trust Maturity Model implementation details
- Conditional Access engineering
- Microsegmentation engineering
- Identity analytics implementation
- Continuous monitoring implementation
- AI-assisted Zero Trust automation

Those concepts are owned or extended in later modules.

**Federal / DoD Operational Perspective**

Module 3 examines enterprise identity infrastructure as mission-critical technical infrastructure across federal civilian agencies, Defense Agencies, Combatant Commands, Intelligence Community elements, military departments, and mission-partner environments.

The operational thesis is that Active Directory and supporting identity infrastructure are not merely administrative conveniences. They determine whether personnel can authenticate, whether mission systems can authorize access, whether Commands can trust identities across disparate domains and forests, whether privileged administrators can manage critical resources, whether certificate-based authentication functions, whether federation works across mission partners, and whether operational continuity can be maintained during degraded conditions.

In federal and defense environments, failures in DNS, Kerberos, LDAP, Domain Controller availability, PKI, AD CS, federation services, replication, Group Policy, or hybrid identity synchronization can create mission impact far beyond routine information technology disruption. This module frames enterprise identity infrastructure as the technical backbone of mission assurance.

**Transition**

Having established how enterprise identity infrastructure technically operates and functions, the reader is now prepared to examine how that infrastructure is assessed responsibly. Module 4 transitions from technical architecture into professional assessment methodology by introducing Active Directory and FICAM security assessment planning, scoping, Rules of Engagement (RoE), authorization boundaries, mission objectives, identity attack surface analysis, threat modeling, evidence and artifact collection, MITRE ATT&CK framework mapping, executive-level reporting, and operational preparation for authorized identity-focused security assessments and audits.

## ~~OLD: Chapter 3~~ **~~v1.0~~** ~~- Enterprise Identity Infrastructure: Networking, Directory Services, Authentication Protocols, and Active Directory Architecture~~

**Operational Objectives**

**Chapter Purpose**

Chapter 3 establishes the technical foundation of enterprise identity infrastructures by examining the networking, directory services, authentication protocols, and architectural components that enable Active Directory to operate within modern enterprise environments. Readers develop an understanding of how identity information flows throughout a network, how authentication requests are processed, and how enterprise directory services integrate with supporting infrastructure. This knowledge provides the technical framework necessary for understanding Active Directory assessments, offensive operations, and defensive monitoring presented throughout the remainder of the book.

**Chapter Abstract**

Enterprise identity infrastructures rely upon far more than a directory service alone. Active Directory functions as the central identity platform within a complex ecosystem consisting of Domain Name System (DNS), Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP), Lightweight Directory Access Protocol (LDAP), Kerberos, Public Key Infrastructure (PKI), certificate services, Group Policy, replication, trusts, and enterprise networking. Each component contributes to the authentication and authorization processes that enable users, systems, and services to securely access enterprise resources.

Chapter3 examines the architecture of Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS), the relationships between forests, domains, trees, organizational units, sites, trusts, domain controllers, and the networking protocols that support identity operations. You will gain a practical understanding of authentication workflows, directory replication, name resolution, time synchronization, and supporting infrastructure while exploring how identity information traverses' enterprise networks.

The chapter concludes by demonstrating how architectural decisions influence both security and attack surface. Understanding the relationships among networking, identity services, and directory architecture prepares you for the assessment methodologies introduced in the following chapter and establishes the technical vocabulary used throughout the remainder of the book.

**Key Terminology**

- Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS)
- Domain Controller (DC)
- Forest
- Domain
- Tree
- Organizational Unit (OU)
- Site
- Trust
- Global Catalog (GC)
- Schema
- Domain Naming Context
- Configuration Naming Context
- DNS
- DHCP
- LDAP
- LDAPS
- Kerberos
- NTLM
- Service Principal Name (SPN)
- Public Key Infrastructure (PKI)
- Active Directory Certificate Services (AD CS)
- Group Policy
- SYSVOL
- File Replication Service (FRS)
- Distributed File System Replication (DFSR)
- Flexible Single Master Operations (FSMO)
- Time Synchronization (NTP)
- Replication Topology

**Chapter Learning Objectives**

Upon completion of this chapter, readers will be able to:

- Describe the architecture of Active Directory Domain Services.
- Explain the relationships between forests, domains, trees, organizational units, and sites.
- Explain the role of DNS within enterprise identity infrastructures.
- Describe Kerberos and NTLM authentication workflows.
- Understand Active Directory replication.
- Explain the function of Global Catalog servers.
- Differentiate logical and physical Active Directory architecture.
- Explain how supporting protocols enable authentication and authorization.
- Identify architectural weaknesses that increase attack surface.

## Special Note: Chapter 3 & Chapter 4 Pivot

After completing Chapters 1-3, I noticed something that I think will improve the flow of the entire book.

Originally, Chapter 4 was conceived primarily as an assessment methodology chapter. I now think it should become the bridge between foundational knowledge and offensive operations.

Instead of focusing only on "how to assess," Chapter 4 should teach readers **how to think like both an assessor (defender) and an adversary before touching a target.** That includes:

- Active Directory assessment methodologies
- Rules of Engagement (ROE)
- Federal authorization boundaries
- Scoping and mission objectives
- Threat hunting and modeling
- Attack path analysis
- Risk-based prioritization
- Lab design and validation
- Mapping assessment activities to MITRE ATT&CK
- Reporting strategies for federal and defense environments

By the time readers finish Chapter 4, they will understand not just the technologies but of the disciplined methodologies that are required to correctly and properly assess enterprise identity infrastructures responsibly. That makes Chapter 5 (Defensive Security Baselines) and Chapter 6 (Reconnaissance) much more impactful and preserves the logical progression I've been building. I believe this adjustment will make the transition into the offensive half of the book exceptionally smooth.

## **🔒Module 4 / Chapter 4 v2.0 - Active Directory and FICAM Security Assessment Methodologies: Planning, Scoping, and Operational Preparation**

**Module 4 Operational Intent & Knowledge Ownership Matrix**

**Module Title**

**Active Directory and FICAM Security Assessment Methodologies: Planning, Scoping, and Operational Preparation**

**Operational Intent**

Establish the disciplined methodology required to plan, authorize, scope, prepare, document, execute, and report identity-focused Active Directory and FICAM security assessments across federal, defense, intelligence, and mission-partner environments.

**Educational Objectives**

Upon completion of this module, the reader should understand how professional identity security assessments are planned and governed before any technical activities occur. The reader will be able to explain how assessment objectives, mission requirements, Rules of Engagement (RoE), authorization boundaries, legal constraints, scoping decisions, threat modeling, identity attack surface analysis, evidence and artifact collection, risk framing, MITRE ATT&CK Framework mapping, and reporting practices shape authorized Active Directory and FICAM-based assessments. This module establishes the operational assessment discipline required before the curriculum moves into defensive baseline validation, reconnaissance, enumeration, adversary operations, detection security engineering, and recovery.

**Identity Lifecycle Phase - Identity Assessment Preparation**

This module owns the assessment preparation phase of the identity lifecycle. It explains how identity environments are evaluated responsibly, legally, ethically, and repeatably before baseline validation, reconnaissance, enumeration, or offensive testing begins. It does not yet teach technical hardening, security baseline implementation, passive reconnaissance tradecraft, active enumeration, credential attacks, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, detection security engineering, or recovery. Those activities are intentionally deferred to later modules. The purpose of Module 4 is to teach the reader how to approach an unfamiliar enterprise identity environment with discipline, authorization, mission awareness, and methodological rigor.

**Adversary Lifecycle Phase - Pre-Operational Assessment Context**

This module intentionally precedes offensive operations. No attack execution, exploitation, credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, detection, or recovery occurs here. Instead, the reader learns how authorized assessors, red teams, purple teams, Security Control Assessors (SCAs), identity engineers, and defenders (blue teams) prepare to evaluate identity infrastructure without creating unnecessary mission risk or disruption.

In addition, this module also introduces Breach and Adversarial Simulation (BAS), attack path analysis, and threat-informed assessment planning at a methodological level only. It does not teach attack execution. Later offensive modules consume this assessment foundation when demonstrating how reconnaissance, enumeration, initial access, credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, and persistence are conducted within authorized scope.

**Primary Knowledge Ownership**

**Module 4 exclusively owns:**

- **Identity-Centric Assessment Methodology.** The structured approach used to evaluate Active Directory and FICAM environments before conducting technical testing, hardening validation, or adversary emulation.
- **Assessment Types and Operational Models.** The distinction among Security Control Assessments (SCAs), penetration tests, red team engagements, purple team engagements, Breach and Attack Simulation (BAS), internal security reviews, architecture reviews, and identity-centric focused posture assessments.
- **Assessment Authorization.** The requirement for explicit authorization, legal approval, command approval, stakeholder coordination, Rules of Engagement (RoE), and operational boundaries before interacting with enterprise identity infrastructure.
- **Rules of Engagement (RoE).** The purpose, structure, limits, restrictions, assumptions, escalation paths, safety controls, and approval requirements that govern authorized assessment activities.
- **Scope and Authorization Boundaries.** The identification of in-scope systems, out-of-scope systems, descoped assets, mission-critical exclusions, production restrictions, maintenance windows, and operational safety limits.
- **Mission Objective Alignment.** The alignment of assessment objectives with mission requirements, operational risk, command priorities, enterprise resilience, and mission assurance.
- **Identity Attack Surface Analysis.** The methodology for identifying identity assets, privileged identities, authentication paths, authorization boundaries, trust relationships, federation points, certificate services, service accounts, administrative pathways, and hybrid identity exposure.
- **Threat Modeling.** The process of developing identity-focused threat models that account for adversary objectives, identity dependencies, trust assumptions, attack paths, mission impact, and defensive control coverage.
- **Attack Path Analysis Fundamentals.** The conceptual use of attack path analysis and identity graph thinking to understand how weaknesses in identity infrastructure can combine into operational risk.
- **Assessment Planning Workflow.** The process for translating assessment objectives into technical plans, team assignments, data collection requirements, tooling decisions, access requirements, safety controls, and reporting expectations.
- **Operational Preparation.** The preparation of lab environments, validation environments, test accounts, instrumentation, logging, evidence storage, coordination channels, deconfliction procedures, and emergency stop conditions.
- **Evidence and Artifact Management.** The collection, preservation, handling, documentation, validation, and chain of custody of assessment evidence and artifacts.
- **Risk Framing and Findings Development.** The transformation of technical observations into actionable findings that communicate likelihood, impact, mission relevance, affected identity components, exploitation paths, and remediation priorities.
- **MITRE ATT&CK and D3FEND Framework Mapping for Assessments.** The use of MITRE ATT&CK and MITRE D3FEND as planning, documentation, detection, reporting, and communication frameworks rather than as exploitation playbooks.
- **Assessment Reporting Methodology.** The development of technical, executive, mission-owner, ISSO, ISSM, system owner, and command-level reporting that communicates identity risk clearly and defensibly.
- **Ethics and Professional Boundaries.** The requirement to conduct all identity security assessments legally, ethically, within authorization, and with respect for operational continuity, personnel privacy, mission sensitivity, and responsible disclosure.

**Knowledge Dependencies**

This module assumes the reader already understands:

- Identity as the foundational security boundary
- Identity as the primary operational battlefield
- Identity as the primary attack surface
- The relationship among identity, credentials, authentication, authorization, accounting, access control, and trust
- The purpose of FICAM and ICAM as federal identity governance models
- The relationship among identity governance, credential governance, access governance, assurance, federation, and mission assurance
- The technical role of Active Directory, directory services, authentication protocols, DNS, PKI, AD CS, AD FS, Group Policy, trusts, replication, Microsoft Entra ID, and hybrid identity
- The high-level identity attack lifecycle introduced in Module 1
- The governance-to-technology relationship established in Modules 2 and 3

This module does not assume prior experience conducting penetration tests, red team engagements, purple team exercises, Security Control Assessments (SCAs), identity graph analysis, BloodHound analysis, MITRE ATT&CK mapping, evidence handling, executive reporting, or federal assessment coordination.

**Knowledge Consumers**

Future modules depending upon this knowledge:

- **Module 5 / Chapter 5**
  - **Identity Security Engineering and Enterprise Hardening**
    - This module consumes Module 4's assessment methodology and applies it to baseline validation, administrative tiering review, privileged access evaluation, STIG / SRG alignment, configuration drift analysis, identity hygiene assessment, PKI governance review, Group Policy hardening review, and continuous validation planning.
- **Module 6 / Chapter 6**
  - **Passive Identity Reconnaissance**
    - This module consumes Module 4's scope, authorization, RoE, and operational preparation model by applying them to authorized OSINT, DNS reconnaissance, certificate transparency review, public identity exposure analysis, federation endpoint discovery, cloud identity footprinting, and external attack surface analysis.
- **Module 7 / Chapter 7**
  - **Active Identity Enumeration**
    - This module consumes Module 4's assessment planning, safety controls, identity attack surface mapping, and evidence practices by applying them to internal enumeration of users, groups, computers, OUs, SPNs, GPOs, ACLs, sessions and shares, trusts, certificate templates, and Active Directory attack paths.
- **Module 8 / Chapter 8**
  - **Initial Identity Acquisition**
    - This module consumes Module 4's authorization boundaries, RoE, deconfliction, and risk framing by applying them to controlled assessment of password spraying, credential stuffing, phishing simulations, OAuth consent exposure, MFA fatigue risk, external remote services, federation entry points, and hybrid identity footholds.
- **Module 9 / Chapter 9**
  - **Credential Acquisition**
    - This module consumes Module 4's assessment discipline, evidence handling, safety controls, and reporting methodology by applying them to controlled evaluation of AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, LSASS exposure, cached credential risk, SAM exposure, Kerberos ticket material, token theft risk, service account secrets, and managed service account exposure.
- **Module 10 / Chapter 10**
  - **Identity Authority Expansion**
    - This module consumes Module 4's attack path analysis, authorization boundary mapping, risk framing, and evidence methodology by applying them to ACL / DACL / ACE abuse analysis, delegation exposure, Shadow Credentials, AD CS template risk, PetitPotam, coercion-assisted escalation, relay chains, and Microsoft Entra administrative role abuse.
- **Module 11 / Chapter 11**
  - **Enterprise Identity Mobility**
    - This module consumes Module 4's scope control, safety planning, mission-risk analysis, and reporting discipline by applying them to controlled assessment of Pass-the-Hash, Pass-the-Ticket, Overpass-the-Hash, NTLM Relay, PrinterBug / MS-RPRN, SMB, PsExec, WinRM, WMI, DCOM, RDP, PowerShell Remoting, remote service creation, cross-domain trust traversal, and cross-forest authentication.
- **Module 12 / Chapter 12**
  - **Persistence and Domain Dominance**
    - This module consumes Module 4's authorization, evidence, risk framing, and mission impact methodology by applying them to controlled analysis of NTDS.dit exposure, DCSync risk, DCShadow risk, Golden Ticket conditions, Silver Ticket conditions, Diamond Ticket risk, Sapphire Ticket risk, Skeleton Key risk, KRBTGT compromise, AdminSDHolder persistence, SIDHistory persistence, rogue certificates, rogue federation trusts, and domain dominance.
- **Module 13 / Chapter 13**
  - **Detection Security Engineering**
    - This module consumes Module 4's assessment documentation, evidence standards, ATT&CK mapping, D3FEND mapping, and reporting methodology by translating assessment activity into detection use cases, telemetry requirements, SIEM correlation, identity analytics, AI-assisted detection workflows, alert enrichment, purple team validation, and attack path disruption.
- **Module 14 / Chapter 14**
  - **Identity Recovery and Domain Trust Restoration**
    - This module consumes Module 4's evidence handling, mission impact analysis, stakeholder communication, and reporting discipline by applying them to post-compromise validation, KRBTGT reset planning, forest recovery, AD CS recovery, certificate revocation, trust repair, Tier 0 reconstitution, credential rotation, and restoration of confidence in enterprise identity infrastructure.
- **Module 15 / Chapter 15**
  - **Operational Lessons Learned from Federal Identity Operations**
    - This module consumes Module 4's assessment methodology by examining real-world assessment failures involving poor scope control, weak RoE, ignored findings, inadequate executive reporting, broken evidence handling, contractor turnover, mission pressure, and identity risk that was documented but not remediated.
- **Module 16 / Chapter 16**
  - **The Future of Identity-Centric Warfare**
    - This module consumes Module 4's methodology foundation and projects it forward into AI-assisted assessment planning, automated attack path analysis, identity exposure management, continuous control validation, adversarial emulation platforms, identity threat detection and response (ITDR), and future federal assessment models.

Module 4 is the assessment methodology dependency for the curriculum because every later operational module requires a disciplined framework for authorization, scope, safety, evidence, mission risk, reporting, and responsible analysis.

**Offensive Ownership**

This module does not own exploitation or attack execution.

It owns the offensive planning and assessment methodology required to ensure later offensive activities are authorized, scoped, deconflicted, documented, safe, repeatable, mission-aware, and defensible. It introduces the reader to Breach and Adversarial Simulation (BAS), threat-informed assessment planning, attack pathway analysis, and operational preparation and awareness as professional assessment disciplines rather than as unsupervised hacking activity.

Specific offensive techniques such as passive reconnaissance, active enumeration, password spraying, AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, AD CS abuse, NTLM relay, DCSync, Golden Ticket abuse, and domain dominance are intentionally deferred to later modules.

**Defensive Ownership**

This module owns the defensive assessment methodology required to evaluate identity security posture before adversaries exploit weaknesses. It establishes that effective defense begins with disciplined assessment planning, validated and authorized scope, mission-aware risk analysis, evidence-driven findings, control effectiveness review, executive communication, and continuous improvement.

This module frames defensive security assessment as a mission-support function rather than a checklist activity. It teaches readers that security assessment results must be meaningful, actionable, traceable, defensible, tied to identity risk, mapped to mission impacts, and communicated to the personnel responsible for remediation, governance, authorization, and operational decision-making.

Detailed technical hardening, detection security engineering, response, and recovery are deferred to later modules.

**FICAM Ownership**

This module applies to FICAM at the assessment methodology level.

**Module 4 owns:**

- Assessment planning for FICAM-aligned identity environments
- Assessment scope definition for identity, credential, access, and federation components
- Evaluation of identity governance controls from an assessment perspective
- Evaluation of credential governance controls from an assessment perspective
- Evaluation of access governance controls from an assessment perspective
- Evaluation of federation and trust governance from an assessment perspective
- Mapping identity findings to mission risk and governance impact
- Assessment reporting for FICAM-related weaknesses
- The relationship between FICAM, assessment evidence, risk framing, and remediation planning
- The use of assessment methodology to validate whether identity governance expectations are implemented and operating effectively

**Module 4 does not own:**

- Foundational FICAM governance
- Detailed FICAM architecture
- Full Active Directory technical architecture
- Security baseline hardening implementation
- Offensive attack execution
- Detection engineering
- Incident response
- Forest recovery
- Future identity modernization

These concepts are owned or extended in other modules.

Later modules may apply, extend, or reference Module 4's assessment methodology, but they do not reteach its foundational approach to planning, scoping, evidence, authorization, and reporting.

**Zero Trust Ownership**

This module applies Zero Trust at the assessment planning and validation level.

**Module 4 owns:**

- Assessment planning for Zero Trust-aligned identity environments
- Evaluation of identity verification assumptions from an assessment perspective
- Evaluation of least privilege from an assessment perspective
- Evaluation of access segmentation assumptions from an assessment perspective
- Evaluation of trust boundaries from an assessment perspective
- Evaluation of continuous monitoring expectations from an assessment perspective
- Mapping assessment activities to Zero Trust identity outcomes
- Translating Zero Trust weaknesses into actionable findings
- Identifying where identity control gaps create mission risk

**Module 4 does not own:**

- Full Zero Trust Architecture implementation
- DoD Zero Trust Reference Architecture implementation details
- CISA Zero Trust Maturity Model implementation details
- Conditional Access engineering
- Device compliance engineering
- Network microsegmentation engineering
- Data security engineering
- AI-assisted Zero Trust automation
- Full detection and response automation

These concepts are owned or extended in later modules.

**Federal / DoD Operational Perspective**

Module 4 examines identity assessment methodology as a professional mission-support function across federal civilian agencies, Defense Agencies, Combatant Commands, Intelligence Community elements, military departments, and mission-partner environments.

The operational thesis is that identity assessments must be planned and executed with the same discipline expected of other mission-sensitive activities. Poorly scoped assessments can disrupt authentication services, affect mission systems, generate false operational alarms, damage trust with mission owners, violate authorization boundaries, or produce findings that cannot be acted upon. Conversely, well-planned assessments provide commanders, system owners, ISSOs, ISSMs, authorizing officials, security engineers, identity administrators, and defenders with evidence-based understanding of identity risk.

In federal and defense environments, assessment methodology must account for authorization boundaries, classification concerns, mission sensitivity, production constraints, contractor responsibilities, operational tempo, change windows, reporting channels, evidence handling, chain of custody, and continuity of operations. This module frames assessment discipline as the bridge between technical identity knowledge and responsible security operations.

**Transition**

Having established how enterprise identity environments are assessed responsibly, the reader is now prepared to examine how those environments should be engineered and validated before adversaries exploit weaknesses. Module 5 transitions from assessment methodology into identity security engineering and defensive foundations by introducing secure baselines, administrative tiering, Tier 0 protection, PAWs / SAWs, privileged access governance, STIG / SRG alignment, configuration drift analysis, Active Directory hardening, Group Policy security, PKI governance, AD CS security, identity hygiene, and continuous baseline validation.

## ~~OLD: Chapter 4~~ **~~v0.1~~** ~~- Active Directory and FICAM Domain and Network Security Assessment Methodologies: Planning, Scoping, and Operational Preparation~~

**Author Note:** I believe this chapter might be one of the most important in the book because it establishes _how professionals conduct assessments._ One thing I want to avoid is making it read like a penetration testing chapter. This is a **methodology chapter**, not a hacking chapter.

Readers should leave Chapter 4 understanding **how to prepare, how to scope, how to think,** and **how to assess** before they ever execute a single reconnaissance command.

I also made another philosophical change.

Instead of calling this chapter "Assessment Methodology," I broadened it to encompass the entire lifecycle of a professional security engagement. That aligns much better with DoD, federal, and contractor practices.

**Operational Objectives**

Teach readers how to properly assess an identity environment before touching anything.

**Chapter Owns**

- Assessment Methodology
- Rules of Engagement (ROE)
- Scoping
- Threat Modeling
- Asset Identification
- **Introduction to BloodHound and Attack Mapping Fundamentals**
- Identity Graph Theory
- Attack Surface Analysis
- Identity Documentation
- **Enterprise Attack Surface Mapping**

**Chapter Purpose**

Chapter 4 establishes the assessment methodologies used to audit and evaluate Active Directory and FICAM implementations across enterprise, federal, defense, and mission environments. Readers examine what it takes to plan, authorize, scope, conduct risk analysis, operationally prepare, and conduct reporting activities that precedes every professional identity-based security posture assessment.

Chapter 4 emphasizes disciplined security assessment methodologies that align technical objectives with mission requirements and expectations, ensuring offensive activities remain legal, ethical, authorized, repeatable, measurable, meaningful, actionable, and defensible throughout the engagement lifecycle.

**Chapter Abstract**

Successful Active Directory Security Assessments (ADSAs) begin long before the first authentication request or network scan or enumerating activity ever begins. Professional security assessments require clearly defined objectives, authorized rules of engagement, comprehensive asset discovery and identification (both physical and logical), mission-partner coordination, threat modeling, and mission-aware planning. Whether conducted as a penetration test, Security Control Assessment (SCA), red team exercise, purple team engagement, Breach and Adversarial Simulation (BAS), or internal security review, security assessing methodologies provide you with the necessary and required structure that transforms technical testing into actionable, meaningful, and usable security intelligence.

Chapter 4 introduces you to the principles of identity-centric security assessment planning by examining engagement scoping, authorization boundaries (e.g., where you are allowed to go, and where you are not / what machines you are allowed to test against, and which ones are restricted from any penetrating activities - which are all defined and agreed upon during scoping and rules of engagement). You will learn how to develop security assessment objectives that align with operational requirements while minimizing mission disruptions and maintaining compliance with applicable legal, regulatory, and contractual requirements and obligations.

The chapter concludes by introducing assessment documentation, evidence and artifact collection, reporting methodologies, and mapping assessment activities to the MITRE ATT&CK Framework, preparing you for the defensive baseline validation activities presented in Chapter 5.

**Offensive & Defensive Tools Discussed**

**Author Note:** This chapter's tool focuses on dual-use cybersecurity tools (e.g., tools that are regularly leveraged by both security professional and adversary; however, are used for different purposes and with different intentions.

<div class="joplin-table-wrapper"><table><tbody><tr><th colspan="2"><p><strong>Primary Dual-Use Tool: Nmap (Network Mapper)<br><em>Network Discovery &amp; Enumeration</em></strong></p></th></tr><tr><td><p><strong>Malicious (Offensive) Use Case</strong></p></td><td><p><strong>Legitimate (Defensive) Use Case</strong></p></td></tr><tr><td><ul><li>Attackers use the exact same tool functionalities to locate high-value targets, build network topology maps, maps attack pathways into the domain network, discovery and identify vulnerable entry points, and attack vectors, and identify outdated/vulnerable software to exploit for malicious reasons.</li><li><strong>Offensive Scripting / Evasion Abuse:</strong><ul><li>Attackers utilize advanced Nmap Scripting Engine (NSE) scripts for automated backdoor discovery or to fingerprint firewall rules. They may also use decoy or spoofed IP addresses or highly fragmented packets to mask their identity from Intrusion Detection Systems (IDS).</li></ul></li></ul></td><td><ul><li>Red teams map attack surfaces, find open ports, protocols, and services, and identify vulnerable service versions. Blue teams use it for legitimate reasons such as to conduct asset inventory discovery, and to audit unapproved open ports, protocols, and services on the perimeter.</li></ul></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2"><p><strong>Primary Dual-Use Tool: Metasploit Framework<br><em>Vulnerability Assessment &amp; Exploitation</em></strong></p></td></tr><tr><td><p><strong>Malicious (Offensive) Use Case</strong></p></td><td><p><strong>Legitimate (Defensive) Use Case</strong></p></td></tr><tr><td><ul><li>Attackers use the framework's massive library of compiled exploits and malicious payloads to weaponize compromised systems, escalate privileges, and execute and inject arbitrary code.</li><li><strong>Evasion &amp; Post-Exploitation Abuse:</strong><ul><li>Threat actors abuse Metasploit modules (e.g., Meterpreter) to bypass Endpoint Detection and Response (EDR) solutions, obfuscate code in memory, and install persistent obfuscated backdoors on a domain network.</li></ul></li></ul></td><td><ul><li>Red teams use Metasploit to safely and responsibly validate whether a known vulnerability can be breached. Blue teams utilize it to recreate exploits (as Proof of Concept (POC)), verifying if their patches and security controls are actually effective and operating as originally intended, and not sporadically deviating from standardized, established, and enforced security baselines.</li></ul></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2"><p><strong>Primary Dual-Use Tool: Burp Suite<br><em>Code &amp; Web Application Security</em></strong></p></td></tr><tr><td><p><strong>Malicious (Offensive) Use Case</strong></p></td><td><p><strong>Legitimate (Defensive) Use Case</strong></p></td></tr><tr><td><ul><li>Attackers abuse its interception and Repeater/Intruder features to conduct automated credential stuffing attacks, brute-force, and modify web requests (e.g., bypassing client-side validation).</li><li><strong>Injection Abuse:</strong><ul><li>Attackers use it to inject malicious payloads - such as SQL injections (SQLi) or deserialization attacks - directly into backend databases to exfiltrate sensitive information.</li></ul></li></ul></td><td><ul><li>Red teams use it for intercepting, modifying, and scanning web traffic to find logic flaws or Cross-Site Scripting (XSS). Blue teams use it to test web application security defenses and analyze suspicious HTTP payloads.</li></ul></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2"><p><strong>Dual-Use Tools: PowerShell, PsExec, and Windows Management Instrumentation (WMI)</strong></p><p><strong>Living-Off-The-Land (LOTL): Malicious- and Legitimate-Native Administration Tools</strong></p></td></tr><tr><td><p><strong>Malicious (Offensive) Use Case</strong></p></td><td><p><strong>Legitimate (Defensive) Use Case</strong></p></td></tr><tr><td><p><strong><em>Living-Off-The-Land (LOTL)</em></strong><em> is an attacking technique where attacker use legitimate, pre-installed system tools (like PowerShell or WMI) to carry out malicious actions. Because these tools are trusted and signed by Microsoft and other reputable vendors, they blend in with normal administrative activities (e.g., "background network noise") and easily bypass traditional av software and other implemented security defense mechanisms.</em></p><ul><li>Attackers abuse these tools to avoid bringing custom malware onto a network, which helps them bypass traditional security controls and trigger security alerts.</li><li><strong>Execution &amp; Movement Abuse:</strong><ul><li>Attackers use PowerShell to download fileless malware directly into memory. They use PsExec and WMI to execute commands remotely, facilitating lateral movement without triggering security alerts.</li></ul></li></ul></td><td><ul><li>Systems administrators and blue teams heavily rely on these native and OS-specific built-in Windows information technology (IT) features, enhancements, and capabilities to manage endpoints remotely, execute patches and deploy remotely, and automate IT operations in general.</li></ul></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2"><p><strong>Dual-Use Tool: Wireshark</strong></p><p><strong>Packet Analysis &amp; Interception</strong></p></td></tr><tr><td><p><strong>Malicious (Offensive) Use Case</strong></p></td><td><p><strong>Legitimate (Defensive) Use Case</strong></p></td></tr><tr><td><ul><li>Attackers with network access use Wireshark and other packet analyzers (e.g., sniffers) to intercept unencrypted data packets that contain plaintext passwords and credentials, or sensitive business data in transit.</li><li><strong>Exfiltration &amp; Evasion Abuse:</strong><ul><li>Threat actors use it to study network traffic patterns, find weak points in the targeted network's encryption protocols, and identify non-monitored segments of the network for easy data exfiltration.</li></ul></li></ul></td><td><ul><li>Blue teams use Wireshark to capture packet data (PCAP) for deep network troubleshooting, network security baselining, and analyzing network-based malware attacker-controlled C2 (Command and Control) communications. Legitimate red teams use it to sniff unencrypted network traffic to sniff out cleartext passwords that may be transmitted over the wire.</li></ul></td></tr></tbody></table></div>

Part II - Identity Assessment and Defensive Foundations

## **🔒Module 5 / Chapter 5 v2.0 - Identity Security Engineering and Enterprise Hardening**

**Module 5 Operational Intent & Knowledge Ownership Matrix**

**Module Title**

**Identity Security Engineering and Enterprise Hardening**

**Operational Intent**

Establish how enterprise identity environments should be securely engineered, baselined, hardened, validated, and continuously monitored before adversaries begin reconnaissance, enumeration, and attack operations.

**Educational Objectives**

Upon completion of this module, the reader should understand how secure identity infrastructure is engineered and validated across Active Directory, FICAM, hybrid identity, and federal mission environments. The reader will be able to explain how identity security baselines, administrative tiering, Tier 0 protection, Privileged Access Workstations (PAWs), Secure Administrative Workstations (SAWs), least privilege, privileged access governance, authentication hardening, Group Policy security, PKI governance, AD CS security, STIG / SRG alignment, configuration drift analysis, and continuous validation reduce enterprise identity attack surface. This module establishes the defensive engineering foundation required before the curriculum moves into reconnaissance, enumeration, initial identity acquisition, credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, detection engineering, and recovery.

**Identity Lifecycle Phase - Identity Security Engineering and Baseline Validation**

This module owns the engineering and defensive validation phase of the identity lifecycle. It explains how identity governance requirements and enterprise identity infrastructure are translated into secure configurations, baselines, administrative models, privileged access controls, authentication policies, authorization boundaries, certificate governance, Group Policy controls, monitoring expectations, and continuous validation practices.

This module does not yet teach passive reconnaissance, active enumeration, initial access, credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, detection engineering, incident response, or recovery. Those activities are intentionally deferred to later modules. The purpose of Module 5 is to teach what a properly engineered identity environment should look like before the curriculum begins the adversary progression in Module 6.

**Adversary Lifecycle Phase - Pre-Adversary Defensive Engineering Context**

This module intentionally precedes adversary operations. No reconnaissance, enumeration, exploitation, credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, detection response, or recovery occurs here. Instead, the reader learns how secure identity environments are designed, configured, validated, and monitored before adversaries attempt to exploit them.

This module prepares readers to understand that many successful identity attacks do not require novel zero-day exploitation. They often succeed because of weak baselines, configuration drift, excessive privileges, legacy authentication, poor certificate governance, weak administrative separation, inadequate access reviews, unmanaged service accounts, and failures to validate whether implemented controls are still operating as intended.

**Primary Knowledge Ownership**

**Module 5 exclusively owns:**

- **Identity Security Engineering.** The discipline of designing, implementing, validating, and continuously improving enterprise identity infrastructure as a secure, mission-supporting control plane.
- **Identity Security Baselines.** The purpose and function of security baselines for Active Directory, Windows Server, identity services, privileged access, authentication policy, Group Policy, PKI, AD CS, federation, and hybrid identity environments.
- **Baseline Validation.** The process of evaluating whether deployed identity infrastructure aligns with approved security baselines, implementation guidance, mission requirements, and risk tolerances.
- **Configuration Drift.** The concept that identity environments gradually deviate from approved baselines because of administrative changes, emergency fixes, legacy systems, contractor turnover, mission pressure, incomplete remediation, and undocumented exceptions.
- **Administrative Tiering.** The design and enforcement of Tier 0, Tier 1, and Tier 2 administrative boundaries to reduce credential exposure, privilege crossover, and identity compromise propagation.
- **Tier 0 Protection.** The identification, protection, validation, and monitoring of Tier 0 assets, including Domain Controllers, enterprise administrators, certificate authorities, federation infrastructure, privileged identity systems, and identity synchronization components.
- **Privileged Access Workstations and Secure Administrative Workstations (PAWs & SAWs).** The role of PAWs and SAWs in separating privileged administration from general-purpose computing, reducing credential theft risk, and protecting high-value administrative workflows.
- **Least Privilege Engineering.** The design of role-appropriate access, privileged access minimization, just-enough administration, just-in-time access, privileged role review, and entitlement reduction.
- **Privileged Access Governance.** The governance of privileged accounts, administrative groups, service accounts, emergency access, break-glass accounts, privileged role assignment, and recurring access review.
- **Authentication Hardening.** The defensive engineering of authentication policies, password policy, Fine-Grained Password Policies (FGPPs), account lockout, protected users, smart card enforcement, phishing-resistant authentication, NTLM reduction, Kerberos hardening, and legacy protocol reduction.
- **Authorization Hardening.** The defensive engineering of access control boundaries, administrative delegation, group membership, authorization models, role design, and privileged access containment.
- **Group Policy Security.** The use of Group Policy Objects (GPOs), security filtering, inheritance, enforcement, linking, delegation, SYSVOL integrity, and policy review as defensive engineering mechanisms.
- **PKI and AD CS Governance.** The defensive engineering and validation of enterprise certificate authorities, certificate templates, issuance policies, enrollment permissions, certificate lifecycle controls, revocation, and certificate-based authentication.
- **Service Account Security.** The defensive management of service accounts, Managed Service Accounts (MSAs), Group Managed Service Accounts (gMSAs), service principal exposure, password rotation, privilege minimization, and ownership documentation.
- **Identity Hygiene.** The systematic review of stale accounts, orphaned identities, excessive group membership, unused privileges, unmanaged service accounts, weak password settings, inactive computer objects, risky delegation, and outdated trust relationships.
- **STIG / SRG Alignment.** The use of DISA Security Technical Implementation Guides (STIGs), Security Requirements Guides (SRGs), Microsoft security baselines, NIST guidance, FICAM expectations, and mission-specific implementation guidance to validate identity security posture.
- **Federal Baseline Context.** The relationship among NIST guidance, FICAM, RMF, DISA STIGs / SRGs, Microsoft security baselines, CISA guidance, FedRAMP, DoD Cloud SRG, and mission-specific baseline requirements.
- **Continuous Validation.** The process of repeatedly assessing identity configurations, administrative privileges, authentication policy, certificate services, Group Policy, trust relationships, monitoring coverage, and drift from approved baselines.
- **Identity Security Maturity.** The ability to assess whether identity security practices are ad hoc, repeatable, defined, managed, measurable, or continuously optimized.
- **Defensive Baselines as Attack Surface Reduction.** The concept that secure engineering practices reduces the adversary's available attack paths before reconnaissance, enumeration, and exploitation begin.

**Knowledge Dependencies**

This module assumes the reader already understands:

- Identity as the foundational security boundary
- Identity as the primary operational battlefield
- Identity as the primary attack surface
- The relationship among identity, credentials, authentication, authorization, accounting, access control, and trust
- FICAM and ICAM as federal identity governance models
- Identity governance, credential governance, access governance, federation, assurance, and mission assurance
- Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS), directory services, Domain Controllers, forests, domains, trusts, DNS, LDAP, Kerberos, PKI, AD CS, AD FS, Group Policy, replication, Microsoft Entra ID, and hybrid identity at an architectural level
- Assessment methodology, Rules of Engagement (RoE), authorization boundaries, scope, mission objectives, identity attack surface analysis, evidence collection, and risk reporting.

This module does not assume prior hands-on experience implementing DISA STIGs, validating Microsoft security baselines, designing administrative tiers, deploying PAWs / SAWs, hardening Kerberos, reducing NTLM, securing AD CS, managing privileged access programs, conducting baseline drift analysis, or building continuous identity validation programs.

**Knowledge Consumers**

Future modules depending upon this knowledge:

- **Module 6 / Chapter 6**
  - **Passive Identity Reconnaissance**
    - This module consumes Module 5's baseline and hardening concepts by showing how external exposure, public records, certificate transparency artifacts, federation metadata, cloud identity indicators, naming conventions, leaked documentation, and public identity signals may reveal whether identity infrastructure is well engineered or poorly governed.
- **Module 7 / Chapter 7**
  - **Active Identity Enumeration**
    - This module consumes Module 5's identity hygiene, administrative tiering, privileged access, GPO security, service account, trust, delegation, and certificate governance foundations by applying them to internal discovery of users, groups, computers, OUs, SPNs, ACLs, sessions, shares, certificate templates, privileged relationships, and attack paths.
- **Module 8 / Chapter 8**
  - **Initial Identity Acquisition**
    - This module consumes Module 5's authentication hardening, password policy, MFA, phishing-resistant authentication, federation security, remote access exposure, and conditional access foundations by applying them to password spraying, credential stuffing, phishing, OAuth consent exposure, MFA fatigue, external remote services, AD FS entry points, Microsoft Entra ID exposure, and hybrid identity footholds.
- **Module 9 / Chapter 9**
  - **Credential Acquisition**
    - This module consumes Module 5's credential hygiene, service account security, protected users, NTLM reduction, Kerberos hardening, LSASS protection, credential guard concepts, password policy, and managed service account foundations by applying them to AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, LSASS exposure, cached credential risk, SAM exposure, Kerberos ticket material, token theft risk, service account secrets, and managed service account exposure.
- **Module 10 / Chapter 10**
  - **Identity Authority Expansion**
    - This module consumes Module 5's administrative tiering, least privilege, authorization hardening, privileged access governance, delegation review, AD CS governance, and certificate template validation foundations by applying them to ACL / DACL / ACE abuse, delegation abuse, Resource-Based Constrained Delegation (RBCD), Shadow Credentials, AD CS template abuse, PetitPotam, coercion-assisted escalation, relay chains, and Microsoft Entra administrative role abuse.
- **Module 11 / Chapter 11**
  - **Enterprise Identity Mobility**
    - This module consumes Module 5's segmentation, administrative separation, remote administration hardening, NTLM reduction, privileged access control, endpoint administration boundaries, and trust review foundations by applying them to Pass-the-Hash, Pass-the-Ticket, Overpass-the-Hash, NTLM Relay, PrinterBug / MS-RPRN, SMB, PsExec, WinRM, WMI, DCOM, RDP, PowerShell Remoting, remote service creation, cross-domain trust traversal, and cross-forest authentication.
- **Module 12 / Chapter 12**
  - **Persistence and Domain Dominance**
    - This module consumes Module 5's Tier 0 protection, KRBTGT awareness, Domain Controller hardening, PKI governance, AD CS security, Group Policy security, privileged access review, service account control, and baseline validation foundations by applying them to NTDS.dit extraction, DCSync, DCShadow, Golden Ticket, Silver Ticket, Diamond Ticket, Sapphire Ticket, Skeleton Key, KRBTGT compromise, AdminSDHolder persistence, SIDHistory persistence, rogue certificates, rogue federation trusts, and domain dominance.
- **Module 13 / Chapter 13**
  - **Detection Security Engineering**
    - This module consumes Module 5's baseline validation, configuration drift, administrative tiering, authentication hardening, identity hygiene, and continuous validation concepts by translating them into detection use cases, SIEM correlation, Kerberos anomaly detection, AD CS abuse detection, privilege change monitoring, lateral movement detection, persistence detection, identity analytics, AI-assisted detection workflows, alert enrichment, and attack path disruption.
- **Module 14 / Chapter 14**
  - **Identity Recovery and Domain Trust Restoration**
    - This module consumes Module 5's hardening, baseline, Tier 0 protection, privileged access governance, PKI governance, credential hygiene, and continuous validation concepts by applying them to KRBTGT reset strategy, forest recovery, AD CS recovery, certificate revocation, trust repair, account recertification, credential rotation, Tier 0 reconstitution, and restoration of confidence in enterprise identity infrastructure.
- **Module 15 / Chapter 15**
  - **Operational Lessons Learned from Federal Identity Operations**
    - This module consumes Module 5's defensive engineering foundation by examining real-world failures involving baseline drift, STIGs checked off but not implemented, excessive delegation, forgotten privileged accounts, shared administrative credentials, broken PKI implementations, weak service account governance, contractor turnover, mission pressure, and identity program drift.
- **Module 16 / Chapter 16**
  - **The Future of Identity-Centric Warfare**
    - This module consumes Module 5's engineering and baseline foundation and projects it forward into continuous control validation, identity exposure management, identity threat detection and response (ITDR), AI-assisted defense, AI-assisted attack path analysis, automated baseline validation, cloud-native identity governance, passwordless authentication, FIDO2, Zero Trust modernization, and future federal identity architectures.

Module 5 is the defensive engineering dependency for the curriculum because every later operational module examines identity either as an engineered control plane, hardened baseline, drift-prone environment, adversary target, detection source, recovery object, or modernization challenge.

**Offensive Ownership**

This module does not own offensive execution or attack tradecraft.

It owns the offensive assessment premise that many identity attacks succeed because defensive engineering failed before the attack began. Weak baselines, privilege sprawl, misconfigured delegation, poor service account governance, exposed certificate services, legacy authentication, excessive administrative access, weak Group Policy control, stale accounts, unmanaged trusts, and configuration drift create the conditions later exploited during reconnaissance, enumeration, initial access, credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, and domain dominance.

Specific offensive techniques such as passive reconnaissance, active enumeration, password spraying, AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, AD CS abuse, NTLM Relay, DCSync, Golden Ticket abuse, and NTDS.dit extraction are intentionally deferred to later modules.

**Defensive Ownership**

This module owns the defensive engineering foundation for identity security.

**Module 5 owns:**

- Identity security baseline development
- Identity security baseline validation
- Administrative tiering
- Tier 0 protection
- PAW / SAW strategy
- Privileged access governance
- Least privilege engineering
- Authentication hardening
- Authorization hardening
- Group Policy hardening
- PKI governance
- AD CS defensive validation
- Service account security
- Identity hygiene
- Configuration drift analysis
- STIG / SRG alignment
- Microsoft security baseline alignment
- NIST and RMF alignment from a defensive engineering perspective
- Continuous validation
- Identity security maturity measurement
- Defensive hardening as attack surface reduction

This module frames identity defense as engineering discipline, not tool deployment or compliance paperwork. Readers learn that secure identity environments must be designed, validated, monitored, and continuously improved. Detailed detection engineering, incident response, forest recovery, and future modernization are deferred to later modules.

**FICAM Ownership**

This module applies to FICAM at the defensive engineering and baseline validation level.

**Module 5 owns:**

- Engineering controls that support identity governance
- Engineering controls that support credential governance
- Engineering controls that support access governance
- Privileged access governance implementation concepts
- Authentication control validation
- Authorization control validation
- Credential lifecycle control validation
- Access lifecycle control validation

- Identity hygiene as a governance control
- Continuous monitoring as a FICAM support function
- Mapping identity security baselines to FICAM expectations
- Translating FICAM governance into enforceable Active Directory, PKI, Group Policy, privileged access, and hybrid identity controls

**Module 5 does not own:**

- Foundational FICAM governance
- Detailed FICAM architecture
- Full Active Directory architecture
- Assessment methodology
- Passive reconnaissance
- Active enumeration
- Offensive attack execution

- Future identity modernization

Those concepts are owned or extended in other modules.

Later modules may apply, extend, or reference Module 5's engineering and baseline concepts, but they do not reteach the defensive engineering foundation.

**Zero Trust Ownership**

This module applies Zero Trust at the defensive engineering and baseline validation level.

**Module 5 owns:**

- Engineering is the least privilege into enterprise identity environments
- Engineering privileged access separation
- Engineering Tier 0 protection
- Engineering stronger authentication
- Engineering reduced implicit trust
- Engineering access control boundaries
- Engineering identity hygiene

**Module 5 does not own:**

- Full Zero Trust Architecture implementation
- DoD Zero Trust Reference Architecture implementation details

- Conditional Access engineering in depth
- Device compliance engineering in depth
- Network microsegmentation engineering in depth
- Data security engineering
- Full detection and response automation
- AI-assisted Zero Trust automation

Those concepts are owned or extended in later modules.

**Federal / DoD Operational Perspective**

Module 5 examines identity security engineering as a mission assurance function across federal civilian agencies, Defense Agencies, Combatant Commands, Intelligence Community elements, military departments, and mission-partner environments.

The operational thesis is that identity security failures frequently emerge not from a lack of policy, but from weak implementation, drift, exceptions, incomplete remediation, overprivileged accounts, outdated protocols, fragile PKI, poor service account governance, and administrative shortcuts taken under mission pressure. In federal and defense environments, these weaknesses can affect access to mission systems, privileged administration, authentication availability, federation with mission partners, continuity of operations, and confidence in enterprise identity infrastructure.

This module frames baseline validation and hardening as operational risk reduction. Proper identity engineering gives commanders, system owners, ISSOs, ISSMs, authorizing officials, security engineers, identity administrators, and defenders a measurable way to determine whether identity systems are configured to support mission assurance rather than merely documented as compliant.

**Transition**

Having established what a properly engineered and hardened identity environment should look like, the reader is now prepared to examine how adversaries and assessors begin learning about that environment from the outside. Module 6 transitions from defensive baseline validation into passive identity reconnaissance by introducing OSINT, DNS reconnaissance, WHOIS and registration data, Autonomous System Number (ASN) analysis, certificate transparency, public GitHub exposure, LinkedIn and personnel discovery, Shodan, Google dorking, public cloud identity indicators, federation metadata, and threat intelligence collection performed before direct interaction with the internal enterprise identity infrastructure.

## ~~OLD: Chapter 5~~ **~~v0.1~~** ~~- Identity Security Engineering and Enterprise Hardening~~

**Author Note:** Chapter 5 begins the book's transition from **building the foundation** to **evaluating the foundation.**

Chapter 5 aims to answer one question: _"How do I determine whether an enterprise identity infrastructure is secure before an adversary does?"_

This chapter will teach the reader the importance of baseline patterning and validation, preparing them for offensive operations beginning in Chapter 6.

**Operational Objectives**

Teach readers how enterprise identity should be engineered before discussing attacks.

**Chapter Owns**

- Identity Security Engineering
- Administrative Tiering
  - PAWs/SAWs, Tiers0/1/2
- Principle of Least Privilege
- STIGs
- SRGs
- USGCB
- FedRAMP
- NIST SP 800 Series Special Publications
- DoD Cloud SRG
- Secure Baseline Configurations
- Administrative Models(?)
- Baselines(?)
- Identity Architecture
- Enterprise Hardening

**Chapter Purpose**

Chapter 5 introduces the principles and methodologies used to establish, assess, and validate secure identity baselines across Active Directory and Federal Identity, Credential, and Access Management (FICAM) environments. Readers learn how properly configured identity infrastructures reduce attack surface, improve mission resilience, and provide measurable security benchmarks prior to offensive assessments. The chapter emphasizes proactive validation, enabling administrators, assessors, and security professionals to identify architectural weaknesses before adversaries exploit them.

**Chapter Abstract**

Security identity infrastructures are built upon well-defined baselines that govern authentication, authorization, directory services, privileged access, Group Policy, certificate services, and identity governance. Without standardized security baselines, enterprise identity environments gradually accumulate configuration drift, excessive privileges (e.g., privilege creep), legacy protocols, misconfigured trust relationships, and undocumented changes and/or the lack of a Change Configuration Control and Management Board (CCCMB) that significantly increase operational risk and threatens Combatant Command readiness postures. Establishing and continuously validating secure baselines is therefore one of the most effective methods for strengthening enterprise identity security posture.

In Chapter 5, you will examine security baseline and benchmark development through the lens of Active Directory and FICAM by integrating Microsoft's security guidance with NIST Special Publication (SP) guidance, DISA Security Technical Implementation Guides (STIGs), Security Requirements Guides (SRGs), and Zero Trust mandated principles. You will evaluate administrative tiering, privileged account governance, authentication hardening, Group Policy management, Federal Public Key Infrastructure (FPKI), Active Directory Certificate Services (AD CS), identity lifecycle controls, and continuous monitoring strategies that collectively define a secure enterprise identity infrastructure.

The chapter concludes by demonstrating how defensive security baselines and benchmarks directly influence offensive assessment outcomes. You will discover that many successful attacks exploit deviations form established security baselines rather than previously unknown vulnerabilities, known as Zero Days, providing a natural transition into reconnaissance and intelligence gathering and collection methodologies introduced to you in Chapter 6.

**Key Terminology**

- Baseline Patterning and Drift
- Secure Hardening Configurations
- Security Technical Implementation Guide (STIG)
- Microsoft Security Baseline
- Administrative Tiering
  - Tier 0
  - Tier 1
  - Tier 2
- Principle of Least Privilege
- Privileged Access Workstation (PAW)
- Privileged Access Management (PAM)
- Just Enough Administration (JEA)
- Just-in-Time (JIT) Administration
- Protected Users Group
- Authentication Policy
- Group Policy Object (GPO)
- Fine-Grained Password Policy (FGPP)
- Identity Hygiene

**Chapter Learning Objectives**

Upon completion, readers will be able to:

- Explain the importance of identity security baselines
- Differentiate Microsoft, DISA, NIST, and USGCB security guidance
- Assess Active Directory against established security baselines
- Monitor for security baseline deviations
- Identify configuration drift and related anomalies
- Implement administrative and authorized secure configurations
- Validate Group Policy security settings
- Assess certificate services and FPKI implementations
- Develop continuous secure baseline validation processes
