**PART III - Identity Reconnaissance & Discovery**

## **🔒Module 6 / Chapter 6 v2.0 - Passive Identity Reconnaissance**

**Module 6 Operational Intent & Knowledge Ownership Matrix**

**Module Title**

**Passive Identity Reconnaissance**

**Operational Intent**

Establish how adversaries, assessors, and defenders develop an external understanding of an enterprise identity environment through lawful, authorized, passive, and open-source collection before direct interaction with internal Active Directory, FICAM, or hybrid identity infrastructure occurs.

**Educational Objectives**

Upon completion of this module, the reader should understand how reconnaissance is the precursor to any well-planned attack. Known as the first pre-attack phase (and with context to identity), identity-based reconnaissance begins before authentication, enumeration, exploitation, or internal access (initial access). The reader will be able to explain how public records, DNS data, WHOIS, and registrar information, Autonomous System Number (ASN) data, Certificate Transparency logs, public code repositories, exposed documentation and leaked secrets, professional networking platforms, search engine indexing, cloud identity indicators, federation metadata, breached credential exposure, public threat intelligence, and external attack surface signals can reveal meaningful information about the target enterprise identity infrastructure.

This module teaches the reader how passive reconnaissance supports both offensive planning and defensive exposure management while remaining distinct from active enumeration. It establishes the first operational phase of the identity attack chain by showing how publicly available information can expose identity architecture, authentication pathways, federation relationships, naming conventions, personnel Personally Identifiable Information (PII), technology stacks, mission partners, and potential weaknesses before the assessor or adversary touches the target environment.

**Identity Lifecycle Phases - External Identity Exposure**

This module owns the external exposure phase of the identity attack lifecycle. It explains how identities, credentials, authentication infrastructure, federation relationships, domain names, certificates, personnel data, cloud services, public repositories, and organizational metadata can become visible outside the enterprise boundary (either intentionally or unintentionally).

This module does not yet teach active internal enumeration, LDAP queries, BloodHound collection, authenticated directory discovery, password attacks, credential theft, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, detection security engineering, or recovery. Those activities are intentionally deferred to later modules. The purpose of Module 6 is to teach how an enterprise identity environment can be understood from the outside before the curriculum advances into active identity enumeration in Module 7.

**Adversary Lifecycle Phase - Passive Reconnaissance**

This module begins with the adversary attack chain progression. It owns the passive reconnaissance pre-attack phase of the identity attack chain, where information is collected without directly interacting with or authenticating to, querying, scanning, or manipulation of internal enterprise identity systems.

This module introduces the reader to reconnaissance as a disciplined intelligence activity rather than as random information searching. Readers will learn how passive information and intelligence gathering supports target understanding, attack path hypotheses development, identity exposure analysis, infrastructure attack pathway mapping, and defensive external attack surface management. Active enumeration, direct service interaction, credential attacks, exploitation, and post-compromise activities are intentionally deferred to later modules.

**Primary Knowledge Ownership**

**Module 6 exclusively owns:**

- **Passive Identity Reconnaissance.** The collection and analysis of publicly available information reveal identity infrastructure, authentication surfaces, federation relationships, external services, personnel patterns, public credentials, and enterprise technology exposure without direct interaction with targeted infrastructure or its internal systems.
- **OSINT for Identity Security.** The use of Open-Source Intelligence (OSINT) to understand targeted identity environments, public attack surfaces, domain ownership, external services, cloud presence, federation exposure, public documentation, exposed code and passwords, and organizational structures.
- **DNS Reconnaissance.** The passive review of domain names, subdomain identification, naming conventions, mail exchange records (MX), service records (SRV), name servers (NS), public resolution behaviors, and external identity-related hostnames and information.
- **WHOIS and Registration Data.** The analysis of DNS registrar data, domain ownership patterns, historical registration information, administrative contact information, and domain portfolio relationships where lawfully available.
- **Autonomous System Number (ASN) Analysis.** The review of ASN ownership, public network ranges, routing relationships, cloud hosting indicators, and internet-facing infrastructure associations.
- **Certificate Transparency Analysis.** The use of public certificate records to identify domains, subdomains, certificate authorities, certificate naming patterns, identity portals, federation endpoints, remote access systems, and cloud service exposures.
- **Public Repository Exposure - Identification and Data Extraction:** Gathering and collection of publicly accessible code repositories, configuration files tied to critical systems, internal documentation, operational scripts, domain names, credentials, secrets, tokens, certificate references, authenticating endpoints, and identity-related artifacts exposed through public version control platforms (e.g., GitHub), open directories, or misconfigured artifact stores. This reconnaissance vector frequently yields hardcoded credentials, infrastructure blueprints, and authentication pathway details that enable direct initial access or credential-based lateral movement against federal and military enterprise identity infrastructure.
- **Personnel and Role Discovery.** Systematic collection and analysis of publicly available professional information to map organizational structure, identify privileged administrator roles, locate identity and access management teams, determine technology ownership and maintenance responsibilities, chart mission-partner relationships, and infer likely operational functions. This foundational reconnaissance enables targeting prioritization, social engineering preparation, and the identification of high-value personnel whose compromise would yield cascading access across the enterprise identity infrastructure.
- **Search Engine Intelligence.** The lawful use of indexed publicly available content to identify exposed documents, login portals, SSH secrets, metadata, technology references, mission partners, domain patterns, public configuration artifacts, and external identity services.
- **Public Cloud Identity Indicators.** The identification of externally visible Microsoft Entra ID, AD FS, federation, SaaS, cloud tenant, and hybrid identity indicators via public metadata and open-source avenues.
- **Federation Metadata Exposure.** The recognition of public federation endpoints, identity provider references, SAML metadata, OpenID Connect (OIDC) metadata, OAuth application exposure, and external trust indicators.
- **Threat Intelligence Collection.** The use of public threat intelligence, breach reporting, malware reporting, paste exposure, dark web reporting, vendor advisories, and external attack surface data to understand identity-related risk.
- **Breach and Credential Exposure Awareness.** The concept that exposed credentials, historical leaks, reused passwords, public email patterns, and compromised accounts can inform both adversarial planning efforts and defensive exposure management.
- **External Attack Surface Management.** The defender's use of passive reconnaissance to identify public identity exposures, unmanaged, unaccounted-for assets, forgotten administrative logon portals, expired services, exposed metadata, public certificates, cloud tenant misconfigurations, and mission-partner dependency weaknesses that leak into Command environments.
- **Reconnaissance Hypothesis Development.** The process of turning passive findings into structured hypotheses about identity architecture, authentication flows, technology stacks, privileged personnel, federated paths, and likely internal dependencies.
- **Passive Reconnaissance Ethics and Boundaries.** The legal, ethical, contractual, and operational boundaries governing passive reconnaissance, information gathering, footprinting, fingerprinting, scanning, and enumeration, especially within federal, defense, intelligence, and mission-partner contexts.
- **Passive Reconnaissance as Operational Preparation.** The relationship between external intelligence collection, attacker hypothesis development, defender exposure management, active enumeration planning, and later attack-path validation. This module teaches how adversaries think before touching the internal environment, while Module 7 remains the primary instructional home for active internal enumeration.

**Knowledge Dependencies**

This module assumes the reader already understands:

- Identity as the foundational security boundary
- Identity as the primary operational battlefield
- Identity as the primary attack surface
- The relationship among identity, credentials, authentication, authorization, accounting, access control, and trust
- FICAM and ICAM as federal identity governance models
- Identity governance, credential governance, access governance, federation assurance, and mission assurance
- Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS), directory services, Domain Controllers, DNS, LDAP, Kerberos, PKI, AD CS, AD FS, Microsoft Entra ID, federation, trusts, Group Policy, replication, and hybrid identity at an architectural level
- Assessment methodology, Rules of Engagement (RoE), authorization boundaries, scope, evidence handling, risk reporting, and operational preparation
- Identity security engineering, administrative tiering, privileged access governance, baseline validation, Group Policy security, PKI governance, AD CS security, identity hygiene, and configuration drift

This module does not assume prior experience conducting OSINT investigations, DNS reconnaissance, Certificate Transparency analysis, public repository review, external attack surface management, breach exposure review, federation metadata analysis, or threat intelligence collection.

Future modules depending upon this knowledge:

Module 6 is the passive reconnaissance dependency for the curriculum because every later operational module examines identity either as public exposure, external intelligence, attack surface signal, targeting input, defensive monitoring source, recovery concern, or future exposure-management challenge.

**Offensive Ownership**

This module owns passive identity reconnaissance as the first operational phase of the adversary lifecycle.

- External attack surface hypothesis development
- Passive reconnaissance ethics and legal boundaries

This module does not own active scanning, authenticated enumeration, LDAP enumeration, BloodHound collection, credential attacks, phishing execution, password spraying execution, exploitation, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, detection engineering, or recovery.

Specific offensive techniques that require direct interaction with target systems are intentionally deferred to later modules.

**Defensive Ownership**

This module owns passive exposure management from the defender's perspective.

**Module 6 owns:**

- Defensive OSINT review
- External identity exposure monitoring
- Public DNS exposure review
- Certificate Transparency monitoring

- Public credential exposure awareness
- External attack surface management

- Cloud tenant exposure awareness
- Public documentation review
- Personnel exposure awareness
- Public portal discovery
- Threat intelligence ingestion from an exposure-management perspective.
- External identity attack surface reduction
- Defensive validation of what adversaries can learn without internal access

This module frames passive reconnaissance as both an adversary activity and a defensive exposure-management discipline. Readers learn that defenders must understand their public identity footprint before adversaries use it to plan initial access, credential attacks, privilege escalation, lateral movement, or persistence.

Detailed detection engineering, incident response, and recovery are deferred to later modules.

**FICAM Ownership**

This module applies FICAM at the external exposure and reconnaissance level.

**Module 6 owns:**

**Zero Trust Ownership**

This module applies Zero Trust at external exposure and visibility level.

**Module 6 owns:**

- The concept that public exposure weakens trust assumptions before authentication occurs
- The relationship between external attack surface visibility and Zero Trust planning

- The relationship between public identity artifacts and risk-based access decisions

Those concepts are owned or extended in later modules.

**Federal / DoD Operational Perspective**

Module 6 examines passive identity reconnaissance as an operational intelligence and exposure-management concern across federal civilian agencies, Defense Agencies, Combatant Commands, Intelligence Community elements, military departments, and mission-partner environments.

The operational thesis is that adversaries can often learn meaningful information about a mission environment without touching the internal network. Public DNS records, exposed certificates, federation metadata, contractor documentation, public repositories, professional profiles, cloud tenant indicators, remote access portals, acquisition records, breach data, and mission-partner references may reveal how identity infrastructure is organized, what technologies are deployed, who administers them, and where external authentication pathways exist.

In federal and defense environments, passive reconnaissance can expose mission relationships, identity dependencies, remote access patterns, federation pathways, privileged personnel, contractor-operated systems, and cloud identity services. This module frames passive reconnaissance as a serious mission risk because externally visible identity information can enable targeted initial access, credential attacks, social engineering, federation abuse, and later operational compromise.

From the defensive perspective, this module teaches that agencies, Commands, and mission partners must continuously understand what their identity footprint reveals to outsiders. External visibility is not merely a public relations or web hygiene issue; it is an identity security and mission assurance concern.

**Transitions**

Having established how an enterprise identity environment can be studied from the outside, the reader is now prepared to examine what can be learned after authorized interaction with the internal environment begins. Module 7 transitions from passive reconnaissance into active identity enumeration by introducing internal discovery of Active Directory domains, forests, trusts, users, groups, computers, organizational units, Service Principal Names (SPNs), Group Policy Objects (GPOs), Access Control Lists (ACLs), sessions, shares, certificate templates, domain controllers, administrative relationships, and attack path.

## **🔒Module 7 / Chapter 7 v2.0 - Active Identity Enumeration**

**Module 7 Operational Intent & Knowledge Ownership Matrix**

**Module Title**

**Active Identity Enumeration**

**Operational Intent**

Establish how adversaries, authorized assessors and defenders actively discover, map, and analyze internal enterprise identity infrastructure once initial interaction with Active Directory, FICAM-aligned identity services, and hybrid identity environments is achieved - whether through legitimate authorizations, credential compromises, or unauthorized accessways.

**Educational Objective.**

Upon completion of this module, the reader should understand how active identity enumeration reveals the internal structure, relationships, privileges, exposures, and attack paths within an enterprise identity environment. The reader will be able to explain how domains, forests, trusts, users, groups, computers, Organizational Units (OUs), Service Principal Names (SPNs), Group Policy Objects (GPOs), Access Control Lists (ACLs), sessions, shares, certificate templates, delegation relationships, privileged groups, service accounts, domain controllers, and hybrid identity objects can be discovered and analyzed during authorized assessment activity.

This module teaches the reader how attackers think once internal visibility begins and how defenders can use the same enumeration logic to identify exposure before compromise occurs. It establishes active enumeration as the bridge between passive reconnaissance and initial identity acquisition while preserving the boundary between discovery, credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, and persistence.

**Identity Lifecycle Phase - Internal Identity Discovery**

This module owns the internal discovery phase of the identity lifecycle. It explains how identity objects, administrative relationships, authentication surfaces, authorization boundaries, directory structures, certificate services, group relationships, trust paths, policy objects, and privileged access pathways become visible once authorized interaction with the internal enterprise identity environment begins.

This module does not yet teach initial access execution, password attacks, credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, detection engineering, incident response, or recovery. Those activities are intentionally deferred to later modules. The purpose of Module 7 is to teach how enterprise identity infrastructure is mapped from the inside before the curriculum advances into initial identity acquisition in Module 8.

**Adversary Lifecycle Phase - Active Enumeration**

This module owns the active enumeration phase of the identity attack chain. It begins after passive reconnaissance and before initial identity acquisition, credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, and persistence.

This module teaches how an authenticated or otherwise authorized assessment position can be used to understand the internal identity environment. It focuses on discovering relationships, privileges, exposures, misconfigurations, and attack-path conditions rather than exploiting them. The module emphasizes attacker reasoning, defender visibility, and assessment discipline while deferring offensive execution to later modules.

**Primary Knowledge Ownership**

**Module 7 exclusively owns:**

- **Active Identity Enumeration.** The process of discovering and analyzing internal enterprise identity objects, relationships, privileges, policy structures, trust paths, administrative boundaries, and attack-path conditions through authorized interaction with identity infrastructure.
- **Active Directory Enumeration.** The discovery of Active Directory domains, forests, domain controllers, trusts, users, groups, computers, OUs, GPOs, ACLs, SPNs, sessions, shares, delegation settings, and directory relationships.
- **Domain and Forest Discovery.** The identification of domain names, forest structure, domain hierarchy, forest functional considerations, domain controllers, Global Catalog servers, trust boundaries, and enterprise identity scope.
- **Trust Relationship Enumeration.** The discovery and analysis of domain trusts, forest trusts, external trusts, transitive trusts, trust direction, trust type, trust boundaries, and mission-partner identity relationships.
- **User, Group, and Computer Enumeration.** The discovery of identity principals, privileged users, standard users, group nesting, computer objects, workstation patterns, server objects, administrative accounts, disabled accounts, stale accounts, and high-value identity targets.
- **Privileged Group Enumeration.** The identification and analysis of built-in privileged groups, delegated administrative groups, nested administrative membership, Tier 0 exposure, shadow administrators, privileged role assignments, and excessive administrative access.
- **Organizational Unit Enumeration.** The discovery of OU structure, administrative delegation boundaries, policy application patterns, object placement, operational groupings, and governance implications.
- **Service Principal Name Enumeration.** The discovery and analysis of SPNs as indicators of service accounts, application dependencies, authentication surfaces, Kerberos service relationships, and future credential attack conditions.
- **Group Policy Enumeration.** The discovery of GPOs, GPO links, inheritance, enforcement, security filtering, delegation, SYSVOL exposure, policy relationships, and configuration signals relevant to identity security posture.
- **ACL, DACL, ACE, and Permission Enumeration.** The discovery and interpretation of access control relationships, delegated permissions, object control paths, ownership, write privileges, reset-password rights, and administrative exposure conditions.
- **Session and Share Enumeration.** The discovery of logged-on user sessions, accessible shares, administrative shares, shared resource exposure, operational data locations, and relationship indicators relevant to later movement planning.
- **Delegation Enumeration.** The discovery of unconstrained delegation, constrained delegation, Resource-Based Constrained Delegation (RBCD), service delegation patterns, and delegation relationships that may create future privilege escalation or movement paths.
- **Certificate Services Enumeration.** The discovery of Enterprise Certificate Authorities, certificate templates, enrollment permissions, issuance settings, authentication-capable templates, certificate authority relationships, and AD CS exposure indicators without teaching AD CS exploitation.
- **Hybrid Identity Enumeration.** The discovery of Microsoft Entra ID relationships, synchronization indicators, federation relationships, hybrid identity dependencies, cloud identity objects, privileged cloud roles, and identity bridge points where visible within authorized scope.
- **LDAP, SMB, RPC, DNS, and ADWS Discovery Concepts.** The role of common Windows and directory protocols in exposing identity information during authorized enumeration.
- **BloodHound and Identity Graph Thinking.** The use of graph-based analysis to understand relationships among users, groups, computers, permissions, sessions, trusts, delegation, GPOs, and attack paths.
- **Attack Path Analysis Fundamentals.** The interpretation of discovered identity relationships as possible pathways that later modules may use to explain credential access, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, and domain dominance.
- **Enumeration Evidence and Documentation.** The documentation of discovered identity objects, relationships, exposures, attack paths, assumptions, limitations, and validation requirements for assessment reporting.
- **Defensive Enumeration Review.** The defender's use of enumeration methods to identify exposure, excessive privilege, dangerous delegation, weak governance, stale objects, certificate risk, trust sprawl, and attack paths before adversaries exploit them.
- **Enumeration Boundaries and Ethics.** The requirement that active enumeration remain authorized, scoped, documented, rate-limited where appropriate, deconflicted, and aligned with Rules of Engagement (RoE), mission requirements, and operational safety.

**Knowledge Dependencies**

This module assumes the reader already understands:

- Identity as the foundational security boundary
- Identity as the primary operational battlefield
- Identity as the primary attack surface
- The relationship among identity, credentials, authentication, authorization, accounting, access control, and trust
- FICAM and ICAM as federal identity governance models
- Identity governance, credential governance, access governance, federation, assurance, and mission assurance
- Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS), directory services, domain controllers, DNS, LDAP, Kerberos, PKI, AD CS, AD FS, Microsoft Entra ID, federation, trusts, Group Policy, replication, and hybrid identity at an architectural level
- Assessment methodology, Rules of Engagement (RoE), authorization boundaries, scope, evidence handling, risk reporting, and operational preparation
- Identity security engineering, administrative tiering, privileged access governance, baseline validation, Group Policy security, PKI governance, AD CS security, identity hygiene, and configuration drift
- Passive identity reconnaissance, OSINT, public DNS analysis, certificate transparency, public repository exposure, public cloud identity indicators, federation metadata discovery, breach exposure awareness, and external attack surface management

This module does not assume prior hands-on experience with Active Directory enumeration tools, LDAP query construction, BloodHound analysis, attack-path graph interpretation, ACL analysis, certificate template review, session enumeration, trust enumeration, or hybrid identity discovery.

Module 7 is the active internal discovery dependency for the curriculum because every later operational module examines identity either as an enumerated object, discovered relationship, attack path, privilege boundary, movement pathway, detection signal, recovery validation target, or future graph-based security problem.

**Federal / DoD Operational Perspective**

Module 7 examines active identity enumeration as a critical internal visibility and exposure-validation activity across federal civilian agencies, Defense Agencies, Combatant Commands, Intelligence Community elements, military departments, and mission-partner environments.

The operational thesis is that attackers, assessors, and defenders frequently see the same internal identity relationships once interaction with the environment begins. Domains, forests, trusts, groups, users, service accounts, administrative pathways, certificate templates, GPOs, sessions, shares, and hybrid identity relationships can reveal how a mission environment is operated, regardless of how it is documented.

In federal and defense environments, active enumeration can expose forgotten trusts, excessive group nesting, shared service accounts, contractor-created administrative paths, unmanaged certificate templates, stale privileged accounts, cross-enclave assumptions, weak delegation, and identity relationships that create mission risk. This module frames active enumeration as a necessary discipline for defenders because a Command cannot protect identity relationships it has never mapped, validated, or understood.

From the defensive perspective, active enumeration is not merely an offensive activity. It is how identity teams, assessors, ISSOs, ISSMs, system owners, SOC analysts, and mission defenders validate whether the internal enterprise identity environment reflects intended governance, architecture, baselines, and mission requirements.

**PART IV - Identity Adversary Operations**

**Phase I - Initial Identity Acquisition**

## **🔒Module 8 / Chapter 8 v2.0 - Initial Identity Acquisition**

**Module 8 Operational Intent & Knowledge Ownership Matrix**

**Module Title**

**Initial Identity Acquisition**

**Operational Intent**

Establish how adversaries attempt to obtain, misuse, or coerce the first trusted identity inside an enterprise environment by targeting authentication entry points, external access services, cloud identity platforms, federation pathways, weak credential practices, social engineering opportunities, and hybrid identity exposure.

**Educational Objective**

Upon completion of this module, the reader should understand how initial access increasingly occurs through identity acquisition rather than traditional perimeter exploitation. The reader will be able to explain how password spraying, password guessing, credential stuffing, phishing, OAuth consent abuse, MFA fatigue, MFA prompt bombing, external remote services, VPN access, AD FS exposure, Microsoft Entra ID exposure, SAML entry points, hybrid identity pathways, and federated authentication weaknesses can allow an adversary to obtain or misuse a trusted identity.

This module teaches the reader how attackers think the moment they attempt to become "trusted" by the enterprise and how defenders can reduce, detect, and disrupt those attempts through authentication hardening, phishing-resistant authentication, Conditional Access, federation governance, identity protection, external service review, access policy enforcement, and Zero Trust-aligned controls. It establishes initial identity acquisition as the first true compromise phase of the identity attack chain while preserving the boundary between obtaining a trusted identity, acquiring additional credentials, escalating authority, moving laterally, and establishing persistence.

**Identity Lifecycle Phase - Identity Entry and Trust Establishment**

This module owns the identity entry phase of the lifecycle, where an external actor attempts to become accepted by the enterprise identity system as a valid user, application, session, device, or authentication flow. It explains how authentication systems, remote access services, cloud identity platforms, federation services, password policies, MFA controls, OAuth consent flows, and hybrid identity pathways determine whether an identity is trusted at the point of entry.

This module does not yet teach credential harvesting after access, AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, LSASS credential dumping, cached credential extraction, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, detection engineering, incident response, or recovery. Those activities are intentionally deferred to later modules. The purpose of Module 8 is to teach how adversaries obtain or misuse the first trusted identity before the curriculum advances into credential acquisition in Module 9.

**Adversary Lifecycle Phase - Initial Identity Acquisition**

This module owns the initial identity acquisition phase of the identity attack chain. It begins after passive reconnaissance and active enumeration have helped identify users, authentication surfaces, external services, federation pathways, cloud identity exposure, and likely access opportunities.

This module teaches how adversaries attempt to enter the enterprise by abusing identity trust rather than by immediately exploiting internal systems. The focus is on acquiring or misusing a valid identity foothold, not on extracting additional credentials, escalating privileges, moving laterally, or establishing persistence. Those follow-on actions are intentionally deferred to later modules.

**Primary Knowledge Ownership**

**Module 8 exclusively owns:**

- **Initial Identity Acquisition.** The process by which an adversary attempts to obtain, misuse, coerce, or socially engineer the first trusted identity accepted by an enterprise identity system.
- **Trusted Identity Foothold.** The concept that the first successful login, accepted authentication, consented application, federation assertion, or remote access session can create an operational foothold even before privilege escalation occurs.
- **Valid Account Entry.** The use or misuse of valid accounts as an initial access mechanism, including the strategic importance of account legitimacy in bypassing traditional perimeter assumptions.
- **Password-Based Initial Access.** Password spraying, password guessing, credential stuffing, password reuse, weak password policy exposure, and externally exposed authentication portals as initial identity acquisition concerns.
- **Credential Reuse at Entry.** The use of previously exposed, reused, breached, or guessed credentials to obtain the first trusted identity session without teaching internal credential acquisition.
- **Phishing and Social Engineering for Initial Identity Acquisition.** The role of spearphishing, credential phishing, social engineering, pretexting, and identity deception in convincing users or administrators to participate in the adversary's entry attempt.
- **OAuth Consent Abuse.** The abuse of application consent, delegated permissions, user consent, malicious application registration, and cloud identity authorization flows as initial identity acquisition pathways.
- **MFA Fatigue and Prompt Bombing.** The abuse of repeated authentication prompts, user confusion, weak MFA enrollment, push approval fatigue, and poor authentication ceremony design as initial identity acquisition pathways.
- **External Remote Services.** The role of VPN portals, remote desktop gateways, VDI platforms, exposed administrative portals, cloud access portals, externally accessible identity services, and remote work infrastructure as identity entry points.
- **Network Authentication Entry Points.** The role of RADIUS, TACACS+, network access control, administrative authentication services, and remote access authentication dependencies as identity acquisition surfaces.
- **Federation Entry Points.** The role of AD FS, SAML, OpenID Connect, OAuth, claims-based authentication, identity provider metadata, relying party trust, and federation misconfiguration in initial identity acquisition.
- **Microsoft Entra ID Initial Access.** The role of Microsoft Entra ID tenants, cloud authentication, Conditional Access gaps, legacy authentication exposure, application consent settings, external collaboration, and hybrid identity relationships in initial identity acquisition.
- **Hybrid Identity Entry Points.** The role of Entra Connect, synchronized identities, federated authentication, password hash synchronization, pass-through authentication, cloud-to-on-premises dependencies, and hybrid account exposure in initial identity acquisition.
- **Authentication Policy Weaknesses.** The relationship among weak password policy, inconsistent MFA enforcement, legacy authentication, weak lockout policy, bypassed Conditional Access, unmanaged external access, and identity entry risk.
- **Initial Identity Acquisition Hypothesis Development.** The use of passive reconnaissance and active enumeration findings to determine which identities, portals, authentication flows, federation paths, and remote services are likely to become viable initial access opportunities.
- **Initial Access Defensive Control Mapping.** The mapping of initial identity acquisition risks authentication hardening, access policy enforcement, Conditional Access, phishing-resistant authentication, federation governance, identity protection, external service review, and monitoring requirements.
- **Initial Identity Acquisition Boundaries and Ethics.** The requirement that any assessment of password attacks, phishing simulations, remote access exposure, OAuth consent risks, MFA fatigue risks, or federation entry points remain authorized, scoped, deconflicted, documented, and aligned with Rules of Engagement (RoE), mission requirements, and legal boundaries.

**Knowledge Dependencies**

**This module assumes the reader already understands:**

- Identity as the foundational security boundary
- Identity as the primary operational battlefield
- Identity as the primary attack surface
- The relationship among identity, credentials, authentication, authorization, accounting, access control, and trust
- FICAM and ICAM as federal identity governance models
- Identity governance, credential governance, access governance, federation, assurance, and mission assurance
- Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS), directory services, Domain Controllers, DNS, LDAP, Kerberos, PKI, AD CS, AD FS, Microsoft Entra ID, federation, trusts, Group Policy, replication, and hybrid identity at an architectural level
- Assessment methodology, Rules of Engagement (RoE), authorization boundaries, scope, evidence handling, risk reporting, and operational preparation
- Identity security engineering, administrative tiering, privileged access governance, baseline validation, Group Policy security, PKI governance, AD CS security, identity hygiene, authentication hardening, and configuration drift
- Passive identity reconnaissance, OSINT, DNS analysis, certificate transparency, public repository exposure, public cloud identity indicators, federation metadata discovery, breach exposure awareness, and external attack surface management
- Active identity enumeration, user discovery, group discovery, computer discovery, SPN discovery, GPO discovery, ACL discovery, trust discovery, certificate template discovery, session discovery, share discovery, and identity graph analysis

This module does not assume prior hands-on experience conducting password spraying assessments, phishing simulations, OAuth consent assessments, MFA fatigue testing, Conditional Access reviews, federation security assessments, remote access reviews, Microsoft Entra ID exposure analysis, or hybrid identity foothold analysis

**Knowledge Consumers**

**Future modules depending upon this knowledge:**

- **Module 9 / Chapter 9**
  - **Credential Acquisition**
    - This module consumes Module 8's initial trusted identity foothold and applies it to post-entry credential acquisition, including AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, LSASS exposure, cached credential risk, SAM exposure, Kerberos ticket material, token theft risk, service account secrets, managed service account exposure, and credential lifecycle failures.
- **Module 10 / Chapter 10**
  - **Identity Authority Expansion**
    - This module consumes Module 8's trusted identity foothold by applying it to authorization abuse, ACL / DACL / ACE abuse, delegation abuse, Resource-Based Constrained Delegation (RBCD), Shadow Credentials, AD CS template abuse, PetitPotam, coercion-assisted escalation, relay chains, Microsoft Entra administrative role abuse, and privilege governance failures.
- **Module 11 / Chapter 11**
  - **Enterprise Identity Mobility**
    - This module consumes Module 8's initial identity foothold by applying it to lateral movement through Pass-the-Hash, Pass-the-Ticket, Overpass-the-Hash, NTLM Relay, PrinterBug / MS-RPRN, SMB, PsExec, WinRM, WMI, DCOM, RDP, PowerShell Remoting, remote service creation, cross-domain trust traversal, and cross-forest authentication.
- **Module 12 / Chapter 12**
  - **Persistence and Domain Dominance**
    - This module consumes Module 8's initial identity foothold by showing how an initial trusted identity can become the entry point for strategic compromise involving NTDS.dit exposure, DCSync, DCShadow, Golden Ticket, Silver Ticket, Diamond Ticket, Sapphire Ticket, Skeleton Key, KRBTGT compromise, AdminSDHolder persistence, SIDHistory persistence, rogue certificates, rogue federation trusts, and domain dominance.
- **Module 13 / Chapter 13**
  - **Detection Security Engineering**
    - This module consumes Module 8's initial identity acquisition model by translating password spraying, credential stuffing, phishing, OAuth consent abuse, MFA fatigue, external remote service access, federation abuse, Microsoft Entra ID anomalies, Conditional Access bypass indicators, and hybrid identity entry activity into detection use cases, telemetry requirements, SIEM correlation, identity analytics, AI-assisted detection workflows, alert enrichment, and attack path disruption.
- **Module 14 / Chapter 14**
  - **Identity Recovery and Domain Trust Restoration**
    - This module consumes Module 8's initial access model by applying it to post-incident validation of compromised accounts, authentication policy review, password reset strategy, MFA reset and re-enrollment, token revocation, OAuth application cleanup, federation trust review, remote access restriction, Conditional Access remediation, and restoration of confidence in enterprise identity entry controls.
- **Module 15 / Chapter 15**
  - **Operational Lessons Learned from Federal Identity Operations**
    - This module consumes Module 8's initial identity acquisition concepts by examining real-world failures involving weak passwords, inconsistent MFA enforcement, exposed VPN portals, forgotten AD FS endpoints, excessive user consent, unmanaged external collaboration, reused passwords, contractor access, and identity entry paths that were known but not remediated.
- **Module 16 / Chapter 16**
  - **The Future of Identity-Centric Warfare**
    - This module consumes Module 8's initial identity acquisition foundation and projects it forward into passwordless authentication, FIDO2, phishing-resistant identity, AI-assisted phishing, synthetic social engineering, adversarial AI targeting, automated credential stuffing defense, identity threat detection and response (ITDR), continuous access evaluation, adaptive authentication, and future federal identity entry models.

Module 8 is the initial identity acquisition dependency for the curriculum because every later operational module examines identity either as a foothold, credential source, authority boundary, movement enabler, persistence pathway, detection signal, recovery concern, or future authentication challenge.

**Offensive Ownership**

This module owns initial identity acquisition as the first true compromise phase of the identity attack chain.

Detailed detection engineering, incident response, and recovery are deferred to later modules.

**Federal / DoD Operational Perspective**

Module 8 examines initial identity acquisition as a mission-critical access risk across federal civilian agencies, Defense Agencies, Combatant Commands, Intelligence Community elements, military departments, and mission-partner environments.

The operational thesis is that an adversary does not need to "break into the network" in the traditional sense if they can convince identity infrastructure to trust them. A valid account, accepted MFA response, consented malicious application, exposed federation pathway, reused password, misconfigured remote access service, or weak Conditional Access policy can create an identity foothold that appears legitimate to many enterprise controls.

In federal and defense environments, initial identity acquisition may affect access to mission systems, administrative portals, cloud services, remote work infrastructure, coalition environments, contractor-operated systems, and cross-agency federation pathways. Weaknesses in authentication policy, MFA enforcement, federation governance, credential hygiene, external access review, and application consent governance can create mission impact before any malware is deployed.

This module frames initial identity acquisition as a mission assurance concern because the first trusted identity can become the starting point for credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, and domain dominance.

**Transition**

Having established how adversaries attempt to obtain or misuse the first trusted identity, the reader is now prepared to examine how attackers acquire additional identity material after entry. Module 9 transitions from initial identity acquisition into credential acquisition by introducing enterprise credentials as identity artifacts, including passwords, hashes, Kerberos tickets, Ticket Granting Tickets (TGTs), service tickets, service account secrets, LSASS exposure, cached credentials, SAM exposure, token theft, AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, managed service account exposure, cloud tokens, Primary Refresh Tokens (PRTs), OAuth tokens, refresh tokens, and credential lifecycle failures.

**Phase III - Identity Authority Expansion**

## **🔒Module 10 / Chapter 10 v2.0 - Identity Authority Expansion**

**Module 10 Operational Intent & Knowledge Ownership Matrix**

**Module Title**

**Identity Authority Expansion**

**Operational Intent**

Establish how adversaries convert authenticated identities and acquired credential material into greater enterprise authority by exploiting authorization weaknesses, excessive delegation, privileged access governance failures, certificate services misconfigurations, coercion-assisted escalation paths, relay chains, and hybrid identity role abuse.

**Educational Objectives**

Upon completion of this module, the reader should understand that privilege escalation in identity environments is not merely "becoming Domain Admin." The reader will be able to explain how identity authority expands through authorization design failures, excessive permissions, weak delegation, unsafe Access Control Lists (ACLs), Discretionary Access Control Lists (DACLs), Access Control Entries (ACEs), certificate template misconfigurations, Active Directory Certificate Services (AD CS) abuse, coercion-assisted escalation, Resource-Based Constrained Delegation (RBCD), Shadow Credentials, SIDHistory abuse, AdminSDHolder exposure, Microsoft Entra administrative role abuse, and shadow administrator conditions.

This module teaches authority expansion as the phase where authenticated access becomes elevated control. It also teaches defenders how authorization governance, administrative tiering, privileged access management, AD CS governance, delegation review, entitlement review, Entra role governance, and continuous authorization validation reduce the opportunity for adversaries to turn ordinary identity material into privileged enterprise authority.

**Identity Lifecycle Phase - Authorization and Authority Expansion**

This module owns the authorization and authority-expansion phase of the identity lifecycle. It explains how identities gain, inherit, delegate, misuse, or abuse authority across Active Directory, AD CS, federation services, Microsoft Entra ID, privileged access models, administrative groups, access control structures, and hybrid identity environments.

This module does not yet teach lateral movement, Pass-the-Hash, Pass-the-Ticket, NTLM Relay as an enterprise mobility technique, PrinterBug / MS-RPRN as lateral movement, remote service execution, persistence, domain dominance, detection engineering, incident response, or recovery. Those activities are intentionally deferred to later modules. The purpose of Module 10 is to teach how adversaries expand identity authority before the curriculum advances into enterprise identity mobility in Module 11.

**Adversary Lifecycle Phase - Identity Authority Expansion**

This module owns the identity authority expansion phase of the identity attack chain. It begins after reconnaissance, enumeration, initial identity acquisition, and credential acquisition have identified accounts, permissions, delegation paths, certificate templates, administrative relationships, and identity material that may enable authority expansion.

This module teaches how adversaries attempt to convert authenticated access into greater control by exploiting authorization relationships rather than merely stealing more credentials. The focus is on expanding authority, not moving laterally across systems, establishing persistence, or dominating the domain. Those follow-on actions are intentionally deferred to later modules.

**Primary Knowledge Ownership**

**Module 10 exclusively owns:**

- **Identity Authority Expansion.** The process by which adversaries convert authenticated identities, acquired credentials, delegated rights, certificate material, or role assignments into greater authority within enterprise identity infrastructure.
- **Authentication versus Authorization.** The distinction between proving identity and receiving authority, including why successful authentication does not automatically imply appropriate authorization.
- **Authorization Abuse.** The abuse of permissions, rights, role assignments, delegation, group membership, object ownership, and access control relationships to expand authority.
- **Access Control Structures.** The role of ACLs, DACLs, System Access Control Lists (SACLs), ACEs, security descriptors, object ownership, inherited permissions, explicit permissions, and delegated control in Active Directory authorization.
- **ACL / DACL / ACE Abuse.** The abuse of directory permissions and object-level rights to modify users, groups, computers, service accounts, delegation settings, passwords, ownership, or other authority-bearing objects.
- **GenericAll and GenericWrite.** The role of broad object control and write permissions as authority expansion conditions.
- **WriteDACL and WriteOwner.** The role of permission modification and ownership modification as authority expansion conditions.
- **ForceChangePassword.** The role of delegated password-reset rights as an authority expansion condition.
- **Administrative Group Exposure.** The relationship among privileged groups, nested group membership, delegated administrative groups, Tier 0 exposure, and shadow administrator conditions.
- **Shadow Administrators.** The existence of identities or groups that effectively possess privileged authority without appearing in obvious privileged groups.
- **Administrative Tier Boundary Failure.** The authority expansion risk created when Tier 0, Tier 1, and Tier 2 administrative boundaries collapse through shared credentials, cross-tier permissions, unsafe delegation, or weak role separation.
- **AdminSDHolder from a Privilege Perspective.** The role of AdminSDHolder and SDProp in protecting privileged objects and how misconfiguration or abuse can create elevated authority paths.
- **SIDHistory Abuse from an Authority Perspective.** The role of SIDHistory in authorization decisions and how improper governance can enable unauthorized authority inheritance.
- **Shadow Credentials.** The abuse of alternate credential material and key trust relationships to create or assume authority over identities.
- **Delegation Abuse.** The authority expansion risks associated with unconstrained delegation, constrained delegation, Resource-Based Constrained Delegation (RBCD), and service delegation relationships.
- **S4U2Self and S4U2Proxy Abuse Concepts.** The role of Kerberos protocol extensions in delegation-related authority expansion, without turning the module into a Kerberos implementation chapter.
- **AD CS Authority Expansion.** The role of Active Directory Certificate Services as an authority-bearing identity infrastructure component where certificate templates, enrollment permissions, issuance policies, authentication-capable templates, and certificate authority configuration can enable privilege escalation.
- **AD CS ESC Categories.** The conceptual ownership of AD CS escalation categories, including ESC1 through ESC16, as identity authority expansion conditions.
- **AD CS Relay Chains.** The use of relay chains specifically when they result in certificate issuance, certificate-backed impersonation, or authority expansion through AD CS. This module does not own generic NTLM Relay as enterprise mobility.
- **PetitPotam as a Privilege Escalation Primitive.** The role of PetitPotam-style coercion in forcing authentication that may support authority expansion, especially when chained into AD CS or other privileged identity pathways.
- **Coercion-Assisted Escalation.** The role of EFSRPC coercion, DFSCoerce, ShadowCoerce, and related coercion techniques as authority-expansion primitives when chained into relay, certificate, delegation, or privileged authentication workflows.
- **Federation and AD FS Authority Abuse from a Privilege Perspective.** The authority expansion risk created by federation trust misconfiguration, token-signing control, claims manipulation, or privileged federation administration.
- **Microsoft Entra Administrative Role Abuse.** The authority expansion risk created by excessive Entra roles, privileged cloud roles, application administrator rights, directory role assignments, consent governance failures, and hybrid role relationships.
- **Hybrid Authority Expansion.** The relationship between on-premises authority, cloud authority, synchronized identities, federation, Entra Connect, privileged roles, and hybrid administrative control.
- **Privileged Access Governance Failure.** The relationship among excessive privileges, poor entitlement review, stale administrative access, weak role engineering, contractor turnover, emergency access abuse, and authority expansion risk.
- **Authority Expansion Path Analysis.** The interpretation of discovered identity relationships as paths that convert low or moderate privilege into higher authority.
- **Authority Expansion Boundaries and Ethics.** The requirement that any assessment involving authorization abuse, delegation exposure, AD CS risk, coercion-assisted escalation, Entra role analysis, or privilege path validation remain authorized, scoped, deconflicted, documented, and aligned with Rules of Engagement (RoE), mission requirements, legal boundaries, and operational safety.

**Knowledge Dependencies**

**This module assumes the reader already understands:**

- Identity as the foundational security boundary.
- Identity as the primary operational battlefield.
- Identity as the primary attack surface.
- The relationship among identity, credentials, authentication, authorization, accounting, access control, and trust.
- FICAM and ICAM as federal identity governance models.
- Identity governance, credential governance, access governance, federation, assurance, and mission assurance.
- Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS), directory services, Domain Controllers, DNS, LDAP, Kerberos, NTLM, PKI, AD CS, AD FS, Microsoft Entra ID, federation, trusts, Group Policy, replication, and hybrid identity at an architectural level.
- Assessment methodology, Rules of Engagement (RoE), authorization boundaries, scope, evidence handling, risk reporting, and operational preparation.
- Identity security engineering, administrative tiering, privileged access governance, baseline validation, Group Policy security, PKI governance, AD CS security, identity hygiene, authentication hardening, credential hygiene, service account governance, and configuration drift.
- Passive identity reconnaissance, OSINT, DNS analysis, certificate transparency, public repository exposure, public cloud identity indicators, federation metadata discovery, breach exposure awareness, and external attack surface management.
- Active identity enumeration, user discovery, group discovery, computer discovery, SPN discovery, GPO discovery, ACL discovery, trust discovery, certificate template discovery, session discovery, share discovery, service account discovery, and identity graph analysis.
- Initial identity acquisition, password spraying, credential stuffing, phishing, OAuth consent abuse, MFA fatigue, external remote services, AD FS entry points, Microsoft Entra ID exposure, and hybrid identity footholds at a conceptual level.
- Credential acquisition, credentials as identity artifacts, AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, LSASS exposure, cached credential exposure, SAM exposure, Kerberos ticket material, token theft, service account secrets, managed service account exposure, cloud tokens, and certificate-backed credential material at a conceptual level.

This module does not assume prior hands-on experience with ACL abuse, DACL abuse, ACE analysis, delegation exploitation, RBCD analysis, Shadow Credentials, AD CS ESC category analysis, PetitPotam, coercion-assisted escalation, AD CS relay chains, Entra administrative role abuse, or privilege path validation.

**Federal / DoD Operational Perspective**

Module 10 examines identity authority expansion as a mission-critical authorization and privileged access risk across federal civilian agencies, Defense Agencies, Combatant Commands, Intelligence Community elements, military departments, and mission-partner environments.

The operational thesis is that authority expansion is often where an identity compromise becomes a mission compromise. A valid account may initially appear low risk, but excessive delegation, unsafe ACLs, weak certificate governance, shadow administrators, stale privileged roles, contractor-created access paths, poorly governed Entra roles, or inherited administrative rights can transform that identity into a pathway toward mission system control.

In federal and defense environments, authority expansion may affect Domain Controllers, certificate authorities, federation infrastructure, privileged administration, cloud identity roles, mission applications, cross-domain trust, operational networks, and continuity of operations. Weak authorization governance can allow adversaries to bypass intended access boundaries without immediately triggering obvious signs of compromise.

This module frames authority expansion as a mission assurance concern because commanders, system owners, ISSOs, ISSMs, authorizing officials, security engineers, identity administrators, and defenders must know not only who can authenticate, but what identities can control, modify, delegate, enroll, approve, impersonate, or inherit.

- ShadowCoerce
- EFSRPC Coerce
- Microsoft Entra Administrative Role Abuse
- Shadow IT & Shadow Administrators

**Editorial Philosophy**

Privilege escalation is not about becoming Domain Admin - it is about expanding identity authority by exploiting authorization design weaknesses.

**Phase IV - Enterprise Identity Mobility**

## **🔒Module 11 / Chapter 11 v2.0 - Enterprise Identity Mobility and Trust Traversal**

**Module 11 Operational Intent & Knowledge Ownership Matrix**

**Module Title**

**Enterprise Identity Mobility and Trust Traversal**

**Operational Intent**

Establish how adversaries operationalize existing identity authority across trusted enterprise boundaries by using authentication material, remote administration pathways, trust relationships, native Windows capabilities, and identity-enabled access mechanisms to move through federal, defense, intelligence, and mission-partner environments.

**Educational Objectives**

Upon completion of this module, the reader should understand that lateral movement in identity-centric environments is not simply "moving from machine to machine." The reader will be able to explain how adversaries use existing identity authority, credential material, Kerberos tickets, NTLM material, remote administration protocols, trust relationships, shared administrative pathways, service accounts, and built-in operating system capabilities to traverse enterprise identity boundaries.

This module teaches enterprise identity mobility as the phase where previously acquired access, credentials, and authority are operationalized across hosts, services, domains, forests, administrative zones, remote management systems, and mission environments. It also teaches defenders how administrative tiering, segmentation, privileged access controls, remote administration hardening, NTLM reduction, identity-aware logging, trust review, service account governance, endpoint telemetry, and Zero Trust-aligned access enforcement reduce adversary movement. This module preserves the boundary between acquiring credentials, expanding authority, moving laterally, establishing persistence, and achieving domain dominance.

**Identity Lifecycle Phase - Enterprise Identity Mobility and Trust Traversal**

This module owns the mobility phase of the identity lifecycle, where authenticated identities, credential material, delegated authority, administrative privileges, service accounts, remote access methods, trust relationships, and authentication protocols are used to access additional systems, services, domains, forests, or mission resources.

This module does not yet teach persistence, NTDS.dit extraction, DCSync as domain dominance, DCShadow, Golden Ticket creation, Silver Ticket creation, KRBTGT compromise, forest recovery, detection engineering, incident response, or recovery. Those activities are intentionally deferred to later modules. The purpose of Module 11 is to teach how adversaries use identity authority to traverse trusted enterprise boundaries before the curriculum advances into persistence and domain dominance in Module 12.

**Adversary Lifecycle Phase - Enterprise Identity Mobility**

This module owns the enterprise identity mobility phase of the identity attack chain. It begins after reconnaissance, enumeration, initial identity acquisition, credential acquisition, and authority expansion have provided identities, credentials, privileges, sessions, pathways, and trust relationships that can be used to move through the environment.

This module teaches how adversaries operationalize existing access rather than acquire the first identity, collect more credential material, or escalate authority. The focus is movement across identity-enabled boundaries. Persistence, domain dominance, detection engineering, incident response, and recovery are intentionally deferred to later modules.

**Primary Knowledge Ownership**

**Module 11 exclusively owns:**

- **Enterprise Identity Mobility.** The process by which adversaries use existing identity authority, credential material, authentication protocols, administrative pathways, and trust relationships to move across enterprise systems, domains, forests, and mission environments.
- **Identity-Centric Lateral Movement.** The concept that lateral movement is often the operational use of identity trust rather than simple network traversal.
- **Passback Attacks.** The misuse of authentication flows or credential validation paths to move access across systems, services, or administrative boundaries.
- **Pass-the-Hash.** The use of NTLM hash material as reusable authentication material for accessing additional systems without requiring plaintext passwords.
- **Pass-the-Ticket.** The use of Kerberos ticket material to authenticate to additional services or systems without requiring the original plaintext credential.
- **Overpass-the-Hash.** The conversion or use of hash material to obtain Kerberos authentication material and enable Kerberos-based enterprise movement.
- **NTLM Relay as Enterprise Mobility.** The use of relayed NTLM authentication to access another service or system, framed here as movement through authentication trust rather than as credential acquisition alone.
- **PrinterBug / MS-RPRN as Lateral Movement Support.** The role of print spooler coercion and MS-RPRN-style authentication forcing as a mechanism that can support movement when chained with relay or other trusted authentication pathways.
- **SMB-Based Movement.** The role of Server Message Block (SMB), administrative shares, file shares, remote access to services, and Windows administrative pathways in enterprise mobility.
- **PsExec-Style Remote Execution.** The use of service creation and remote administration patterns associated with PsExec-style movement as an identity-enabled mobility technique.
- **Windows Remote Management.** The role of WinRM, PowerShell Remoting, and remote management pathways in identity-enabled movement.
- **Windows Management Instrumentation.** The role of WMI as a remote administration and movement pathway when valid authority exists.
- **DCOM-Based Movement.** The role of Distributed Component Object Model (DCOM) as a remote execution and administrative movement pathway.
- **Remote Desktop Protocol.** The role of RDP access, interactive logon rights, privileged accounts, administrative workstations, jump systems, and remote access policy in enterprise identity mobility.
- **Living-Off-the-Land Administration.** The abuse of native Windows and enterprise administration tools, utilities, management channels, and trusted administrative patterns to blend movement into normal operational activity.
- **Remote Service Creation.** The use of authorized service control pathways to create, modify, or execute services remotely where identity authority permits it.
- **Scheduled Task Abuse for Movement.** The use of scheduled tasks as a remote execution and movement mechanism, without treating scheduled tasks as persistence in this module.
- **PowerShell Remoting Abuse.** The misuse of PowerShell Remoting as an identity-enabled administrative pathway for enterprise movement.
- **Startup and Logon Script Abuse for Movement.** The use of logon scripts, startup scripts, drive mappings, and domain scripting mechanisms as operational movement or redirection pathways, while persistence-focused abuse is deferred to Module 12.
- **Cross-Domain Trust Traversal.** The use of valid identity relationships, trusts, group memberships, authentication paths, and delegated authority to move across domain boundaries.
- **Cross-Forest Authentication.** The use of forest trusts, external trusts, selective authentication, transitive relationships, and trusted identity boundaries to access resources across forest boundaries.
- **Service Account Mobility.** The use of service accounts, application identities, shared administrative identities, and operational accounts to move across systems and services.
- **Administrative Workstation and Jump System Pathways.** The role of PAWs, SAWs, jump boxes, bastions, administrative workstations, and privileged access pathways in either restricting or enabling enterprise movement.
- **Trust Boundary Mapping for Movement.** The interpretation of domain, forest, administrative, remote access, segmentation, and mission-partner boundaries as movement constraints or movement pathways.
- **Movement Pathway Analysis.** The analysis of how credentials, privileges, sessions, trusts, administrative tools, and service relationships combine into operational movement paths.
- **Enterprise Mobility Boundaries and Ethics.** The requirement that any assessment involving lateral movement, remote execution, trust traversal, NTLM relay, ticket reuse, hash reuse, remote administration, or service creation remain authorized, scoped, deconflicted, documented, and aligned with Rules of Engagement (RoE), mission requirements, legal boundaries, and operational safety.

**Knowledge Dependencies**

**This module assumes the reader already understands:**

- Identity as the foundational security boundary.
- Identity as the primary operational battlefield.
- Identity as the primary attack surface.
- The relationship among identity, credentials, authentication, authorization, accounting, access control, and trust.
- FICAM and ICAM as federal identity governance models.
- Identity governance, credential governance, access governance, federation, assurance, and mission assurance.
- Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS), directory services, Domain Controllers, DNS, LDAP, Kerberos, NTLM, PKI, AD CS, AD FS, Microsoft Entra ID, federation, trusts, Group Policy, replication, and hybrid identity at an architectural level.
- Assessment methodology, Rules of Engagement (RoE), authorization boundaries, scope, evidence handling, risk reporting, and operational preparation.
- Identity security engineering, administrative tiering, privileged access governance, baseline validation, Group Policy security, PKI governance, AD CS security, identity hygiene, authentication hardening, credential hygiene, service account governance, and configuration drift.
- Passive identity reconnaissance, OSINT, DNS analysis, certificate transparency, public repository exposure, public cloud identity indicators, federation metadata discovery, breach exposure awareness, and external attack surface management.
- Active identity enumeration, user discovery, group discovery, computer discovery, SPN discovery, GPO discovery, ACL discovery, trust discovery, certificate template discovery, session discovery, share discovery, service account discovery, and identity graph analysis.
- Initial identity acquisition, password spraying, credential stuffing, phishing, OAuth consent abuse, MFA fatigue, external remote services, AD FS entry points, Microsoft Entra ID exposure, and hybrid identity footholds at a conceptual level.
- Credential acquisition, credentials as identity artifacts, AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, LSASS exposure, cached credential exposure, SAM exposure, Kerberos ticket material, token theft, service account secrets, managed service account exposure, cloud tokens, and certificate-backed credential material at a conceptual level.
- Identity authority expansion, authorization abuse, ACL abuse, delegation abuse, AD CS escalation paths, PetitPotam, coercion-assisted escalation, relay chains, Microsoft Entra administrative role abuse, shadow administrators, and privileged access governance failures at a conceptual level.

This module does not assume prior hands-on experience with Pass-the-Hash, Pass-the-Ticket, Overpass-the-Hash, NTLM Relay, PrinterBug / MS-RPRN, SMB-based movement, PsExec-style administration, WinRM, WMI, DCOM, RDP, PowerShell Remoting, remote service creation, scheduled task movement, cross-domain trust traversal, or cross-forest authentication.

Module 11 is the enterprise identity mobility dependency for the curriculum because every later operational module examines identity either as a movement mechanism, trust traversal pathway, administrative channel, persistence bridge, detection signal, recovery validation target, or future access-control challenge.

**Federal / DoD Operational Perspectives**

Module 11 examines enterprise identity mobility as a mission-critical lateral movement and trust traversal risk across federal civilian agencies, Defense Agencies, Combatant Commands, Intelligence Community elements, military departments, and mission-partner environments.

The operational thesis is that once an adversary possesses a trusted identity, usable credential material, or expanded authority, the next question becomes where that identity can go. Shared administrative pathways, unmanaged remote access, weak trust boundaries, service account reuse, excessive RDP access, misconfigured WinRM, exposed SMB pathways, cross-domain trust assumptions, and poorly governed mission-partner connectivity can allow identity compromise to spread across systems, enclaves, domains, forests, and operational networks.

In federal and defense environments, enterprise identity mobility may affect administrative workstations, jump systems, bastions, mission applications, file shares, command-and-control-adjacent infrastructure, operational technology support systems, cloud services, contractor-administered environments, and cross-agency or coalition trust relationships. Movement enabled by identity authority can transform a localized compromise into an enterprise or mission-wide incident.

This module frames enterprise identity mobility as a mission assurance concern because commanders, system owners, ISSOs, ISSMs, authorizing officials, security engineers, identity administrators, SOC analysts, and mission defenders must understand not only which identities exist and what they can control, but where they can move and what trust boundaries they can cross.

**Transition**

Having established how adversaries operationalize identity authority across trusted enterprise boundaries, the reader is now prepared to examine how tactical compromise becomes strategic control. Module 12 transitions from enterprise identity mobility into persistence and domain dominance by introducing NTDS.dit extraction, DCSync, DCShadow, Golden Ticket, Silver Ticket, Diamond Ticket, Sapphire Ticket, Skeleton Key, KRBTGT compromise, DSRM abuse, AdminSDHolder persistence, SIDHistory persistence, malicious Group Policy persistence, long-lived certificate abuse, rogue certificate authorities, rogue federation trusts, long-term service account abuse, domain controller persistence, forest-level persistence, and the collapse of confidence in enterprise identity infrastructure.
