**Phase V - Initial Access, Persistence & Domain Dominance**

## **🔒Module 12 / Chapter 12 v2.0 - Persistence and Domain Dominance**

**Module 12 Operational Intent & Knowledge Ownership Matrix**

**Module Title**

**Persistence and Domain Dominance**

**Operational Intent**

Establish how adversaries transform temporary access, credential material, expanded authority, and enterprise mobility into long-term control over Active Directory, FICAM-aligned identity infrastructure, certificate services, federation trust, domain controllers, and the enterprise identity control plane.

**Educational Objective**

Upon completion of this module, the reader should understand that persistence and domain dominance represent the point at which tactical identity compromise becomes strategic identity control. The reader will be able to explain how adversaries maintain access through Kerberos ticket forgery, NTDS.dit exposure, DCSync, DCShadow, KRBTGT compromise, AdminSDHolder abuse, SIDHistory abuse, Shadow Credentials, malicious Group Policy, long-lived certificates, rogue certificate authorities, rogue federation trusts, service account persistence, Domain Controller compromise, and forest-level persistence.

This module teaches that attacks such as NTDS.dit extraction and DCSync are not merely credential access events. They represent a collapse of confidence in the enterprise identity infrastructure because the attacker may gain access to the material required to impersonate identities, forge trust, recreate authentication artifacts, and survive ordinary remediation efforts. It also teaches defenders how to persistence hunt, Tier 0 protection, replication integrity validation, Kerberos trust reset, certificate authority review, federation trust validation, privileged identity review, and domain recovery planning support restoration of identity trust.

**Identity Lifecycle Phase - Identity Persistence and Trust Dominance**

This module owns the persistence and trust-dominance phase of the identity lifecycle. It explains how adversaries maintain covert access to identity infrastructure, preserve privileged control, manipulate directory objects, abuse authentication trust, compromise credential material at scale, exploit certificate and federation infrastructure, and undermine confidence in enterprise identity systems.

This module does not yet own full detection engineering, incident response execution, forest recovery execution, long-term remediation planning, or future identity modernization. Those activities are intentionally deferred to Modules 13, 14, 15, and 16. The purpose of Module 12 is to teach how identity compromise becomes durable, strategic, and difficult to eradicate before the curriculum shifts fully into detection, response, recovery, operational lessons, and future identity security.

**Adversary Lifecycle Phase - Persistence and Domain Dominance**

This module owns the persistence and domain dominance phase of the identity attack chain. It begins after reconnaissance, enumeration, initial identity acquisition, credential acquisition, authority expansion, and enterprise identity mobility have enabled an adversary to reach high-value identity infrastructure, privileged pathways, domain controllers, certificate services, federation services, and forest-level control points.

This module teaches how adversaries attempt to survive remediation, preserve re-entry options, retain privileged control, manipulate authentication trust, and assume strategic control over the identity environment. The focus is long-term control and trust compromise, not initial access, credential acquisition alone, privilege escalation alone, or lateral movement alone. Detection engineering, recovery operations, and post-compromise trust restoration are intentionally deferred to later modules.

**Primary Knowledge Ownership**

**Module 12 exclusively owns:**

- **Persistence and Domain Dominance.** The process by which adversaries convert temporary compromise into durable control over enterprise identity infrastructure, authentication systems, directory services, certificate services, federation trust, or forest-level authority.
- **Identity Persistence.** The concept that persistence in identity environments often targets accounts, credentials, tickets, certificates, delegation, access control, federation, Group Policy, replication, and trust relationships rather than only hosts or malware implants.
- **Domain Dominance.** The condition in which an adversary can manipulate, impersonate, forge, replicate, or control core identity functions at the domain or forest level, reducing confidence in authentication, authorization, and administrative trust.
- **NTDS.dit Exposure.** The strategic significance of NTDS.dit exposure or extraction as a domain-level compromise condition because it may expose credential material, password hashes, Kerberos keys, and directory secrets required to attack enterprise identity at scale.
- **DCSync as Domain Dominance.** The use of directory replication privileges to obtain sensitive credential material from domain controllers, framed as a strategic trust compromise rather than routine credential theft.
- **DCShadow.** The abuse of directory replication behavior to introduce or manipulate directory changes through rogue or unauthorized replication-like activity.
- **KRBTGT Account Compromise.** The significance of KRBTGT compromise as a foundational Kerberos trust failure because it can enable forged tickets and long-term authentication abuse.
- **Golden Ticket.** The use of KRBTGT-derived material to forge Ticket Granting Tickets (TGTs), framed as domain-level authentication trust compromise.
- **Silver Ticket.** The forging or abuse of service-specific Kerberos tickets, framed as service-level authentication trust compromise.
- **Diamond Ticket.** The manipulation of legitimate Kerberos ticket flows to create forged or modified authentication material with high operational value.
- **Sapphire Ticket.** The abuse of Kerberos ticketing behavior and delegation-related conditions to support advanced authentication manipulation.
- **Skeleton Key.** The concept of authentication backdoors that allow persistent access while preserving or bypassing normal authentication expectations.
- **DSRM Abuse.** The abuse of Directory Services Restore Mode (DSRM) credentials or configuration to support privileged access or persistence on domain controllers.
- **AdminSDHolder Persistence.** The abuse of AdminSDHolder and SDProp to preserve privileged permissions or reapply attacker-controlled access to protected accounts and groups.
- **SIDHistory Persistence.** The abuse of SIDHistory to preserve or inherit unauthorized access across identity boundaries.
- **Shadow Credentials as Persistence.** The use of alternate credential material, key trust relationships, or certificate-backed identity material to preserve access to an identity.
- **Resource-Based Constrained Delegation as Persistence.** The use of RBCD relationships to preserve or regain access through delegated authentication pathways.
- **Malicious Group Policy Persistence.** The abuse of Group Policy Objects, GPO links, security filtering, startup scripts, logon scripts, scheduled tasks, or policy-based configuration to preserve access or execute attacker-controlled behavior.
- **Startup and Logon Script Persistence.** The use of startup scripts, logon scripts, drive mappings, and domain script mechanisms as long-term persistence rather than merely movement support.
- **Scheduled Task Persistence.** The use of scheduled tasks to preserve recurring or delayed execution within identity-managed systems.
- **WMI Event Subscription Persistence.** The use of WMI event subscriptions as durable execution or re-entry mechanisms.
- **Service-Based Persistence.** The use of services, service configuration, service accounts, and service control mechanisms to preserve access.
- **Machine Account Abuse.** The abuse of computer accounts, machine account passwords, machine account quota, computer object permissions, or machine identity relationships as persistence mechanisms.
- **Long-Lived Service Account Abuse.** The use of stale, overprivileged, poorly monitored, or undocumented service accounts as long-term identity persistence.
- **Long-Lived Certificate Abuse.** The use of certificates, private keys, certificate templates, enrollment rights, validity periods, and certificate-based authentication to preserve access beyond password resets.
- **Certificate-Based Persistence.** The use of AD CS, certificate templates, enrollment permissions, private key material, certificate mapping, or authentication-capable certificates to maintain identity access.
- **Rogue Certificate Authorities.** The strategic risk created by unauthorized, compromised, or attacker-controlled certificate authorities that can undermine certificate trust.
- **Rogue Federation Trusts.** The abuse or creation of unauthorized federation relationships, token-signing control, relying party trust, or claims-based trust relationships to preserve or regain access.
- **Federation Persistence.** The use of federation services claims rules, token-signing certificates, relying party trusts, or identity provider control as long-term persistence mechanisms.
- **Rogue DNS and DHCP as Identity Support Persistence.** The abuse of supporting infrastructure when it affects identity operations, authentication routing, service discovery, or mission access.
- **Domain Controller Persistence.** The persistence risk created by unauthorized control, modification, or compromise of Domain Controllers, domain controller configuration, replication behavior, or authentication services.
- **Forest-Level Persistence.** The persistence risk created when the adversary controls or manipulates forest-wide trust, schema, configuration naming context, Enterprise Admin authority, certificate infrastructure, or cross-domain administrative control.
- **Layered Persistence Strategy.** The concept that adversaries may deploy multiple persistence mechanisms simultaneously across accounts, tickets, certificates, Group Policy, replication, federation, services, scheduled tasks, and administrative pathways.
- **Persistence Detection Considerations.** The high-level understanding of what categories of telemetry and validation are required to find persistence, while detailed detection engineering is deferred to Module 13.
- **Persistence Eradication Considerations.** The high-level understanding that remediation requires removing every persistence pathway, while detailed recovery execution is deferred to Module 14.
- **Domain Dominance Boundaries and Ethics.** The requirement that any assessment involving domain dominance conditions, replication abuse, ticket forgery, certificate persistence, federation trust abuse, or domain controller exposure remain authorized, scoped, deconflicted, documented, and aligned with Rules of Engagement (RoE), mission requirements, legal boundaries, and operational safety.

**Knowledge Dependencies**

**This module assumes the reader already understands:**

- Identity as the foundational security boundary.
- Identity as the primary operational battlefield.
- Identity as the primary attack surface.
- The relationship among identity, credentials, authentication, authorization, accounting, access control, and trust.
- FICAM and ICAM as federal identity governance models.
- Identity governance, credential governance, access governance, federation, assurance, and mission assurance.
- Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS), directory services, Domain Controllers, DNS, LDAP, Kerberos, NTLM, PKI, AD CS, AD FS, Microsoft Entra ID, federation, trusts, Group Policy, replication, and hybrid identity at an architectural level.
- Assessment methodology, Rules of Engagement (RoE), authorization boundaries, scope, evidence handling, risk reporting, and operational preparation.
- Identity security engineering, administrative tiering, privileged access governance, baseline validation, Group Policy security, PKI governance, AD CS security, identity hygiene, authentication hardening, credential hygiene, service account governance, and configuration drift.
- Passive identity reconnaissance, OSINT, DNS analysis, certificate transparency, public repository exposure, public cloud identity indicators, federation metadata discovery, breach exposure awareness, and external attack surface management.
- Active identity enumeration, user discovery, group discovery, computer discovery, SPN discovery, GPO discovery, ACL discovery, trust discovery, certificate template discovery, session discovery, share discovery, service account discovery, and identity graph analysis.
- Initial identity acquisition, password spraying, credential stuffing, phishing, OAuth consent abuse, MFA fatigue, external remote services, AD FS entry points, Microsoft Entra ID exposure, and hybrid identity footholds at a conceptual level.

- Identity authority expansion, authorization abuse, ACL abuse, delegation abuse, AD CS escalation paths, PetitPotam, coercion-assisted escalation, relay chains, Microsoft Entra administrative role abuse, shadow administrators, and privileged access governance failures at a conceptual level.

This module does not assume prior hands-on experience with NTDS.dit exposure analysis, DCSync analysis, DCShadow analysis, Kerberos ticket forgery analysis, KRBTGT compromise assessment, AdminSDHolder persistence review, SIDHistory persistence review, certificate persistence review, rogue federation trust review, domain controller persistence assessment, or forest-level compromise analysis.

**Knowledge Consumers**

**Future modules depending upon this knowledge:**

- **Module 13 / Chapter 13**
  - **Detection Security Engineering**
    - This module consumes Module 12's persistence and domain dominance model by translating NTDS.dit exposure, DCSync, DCShadow, Golden Ticket, Silver Ticket, Diamond Ticket, Sapphire Ticket, Skeleton Key, KRBTGT compromise, AdminSDHolder persistence, SIDHistory persistence, Shadow Credentials, RBCD persistence, malicious Group Policy, certificate persistence, rogue certificate authorities, rogue federation trusts, long-lived service account abuse, Domain Controller persistence, and forest-level compromise into detection use cases, telemetry requirements, SIEM correlation, identity analytics, AI-assisted detection workflows, alert enrichment, and attack path disruption.
- **Module 14 / Chapter 14**
  - **Identity Recovery and Domain Trust Restoration**
    - This module consumes Module 12's persistence and domain dominance model by applying it to KRBTGT reset strategy, forest recovery, AD CS recovery, certificate revocation, federation trust repair, Domain Controller validation, replication integrity review, privileged account recertification, service account rotation, SIDHistory validation, AdminSDHolder review, Group Policy cleanup, rogue trust removal, credential rotation, and restoration of confidence in enterprise identity infrastructure.
- **Module 15 / Chapter 15**
  - **Operational Lessons Learned from Federal Identity Operations**
    - This module consumes Module 12's persistence and domain dominance concepts by examining real-world failures involving delayed response to DCSync alerts, excessive replication rights, unmanaged KRBTGT reset planning, certificate persistence nobody found, Group Policy backdoors, forgotten service accounts, rogue trust assumptions, incomplete remediation, and identity compromise that survived incident response.
- **Module 16 / Chapter 16**
  - **The Future of Identity-Centric Warfare**
    - This module consumes Module 12's persistence and domain dominance foundation and projects it forward into identity resilience engineering, cloud-native persistence risks, hybrid identity dominance, AI-assisted persistence detection, adversarial AI persistence planning, continuous trust validation, identity threat detection and response (ITDR), automated recovery validation, Zero Trust modernization, and future federal identity survivability models.

Module 12 is the persistence and domain dominance dependency for the curriculum because every later defensive module examines identity either as compromised trust, durable adversary access, strategic control plane failure, detection challenge, recovery problem, operational lesson, or future resilience requirement.

**Federal / DoD Operational Perspective**

Module 12 examines persistence and domain dominance as strategic identity compromise across federal civilian agencies, Defense Agencies, Combatant Commands, Intelligence Community elements, military departments, and mission-partner environments.

The operational thesis is that persistence and domain dominance are not ordinary incident conditions. When adversaries gain durable control over identity infrastructure, extract NTDS.dit, perform DCSync, compromise KRBTGT, manipulate directory replication, abuse certificate trust, implant federation persistence, or establish multiple re-entry mechanisms, the agency or Command can no longer assume that authentication, authorization, administrative control, or identity trust remains reliable.

In federal and defense environments, persistence and domain dominance may affect mission systems, classified and unclassified resources, Domain Controllers, certificate authorities, federation services, cloud identity tenants, administrative enclaves, mission-partner connectivity, coalition operations, remote access, and continuity of operations. The compromise may survive password resets, endpoint rebuilds, account disabling, service restoration, and partial remediation if the underlying identity persistence mechanisms remain intact.

This module frames persistence and domain dominance as mission assurance failures because commanders, system owners, ISSOs, ISSMs, authorizing officials, security engineers, identity administrators, SOC analysts, incident responders, and mission defenders must understand when identity trust has been degraded to the point that recovery requires coordinated trust restoration rather than ordinary cleanup.

**Transition**

Having established how adversaries preserve access and achieve strategic control over enterprise identity infrastructure, the reader is now prepared to shift fully into the defender's perspective. Module 13 transitions from adversary operations into detection security engineering by introducing identity-centric detection strategy, telemetry requirements, Microsoft Defender for Identity (MDI), SIEM correlation, Kerberos anomaly detection, DCSync detection, DCShadow detection, Golden Ticket detection, AD CS abuse detection, lateral movement detection, persistence hunting, honeytokens, honey credentials, deception, Sigma and KQL detection logic, Splunk detection engineering, UEBA, identity analytics, AI-assisted detection workflows, alert enrichment, purple team validation, and attack path disruption.

**Author Note:** This chapter follows directly after Lateral Movement (Chapter 11). The attacker now has administrative access somewhere inside the environment and shifts from simply moving and pivoting the network to ensuring long-term control, surviving remediation efforts, and establishing multiple methods of re-entry. It also examines how defenders identify, remove, eradicate, contain, and recover from persistence mechanisms before complete domain dominance occurs.

**Chapter Purpose**

This chapter examines how adversaries transition from temporary administrative access to long-term control over an Active Directory environment. Readers will learn how persistence mechanisms are established across identities, authentication services, directory objects, certificates, and domain controllers. The chapter also explores defensive strategies for detecting, eliminating, and recovering from persistence before complete identity compromise becomes irreversible.

**Chapter Abstract**

Obtaining administrative privileges is rarely the final objective of an Advanced Persistent Threat (APT) attacker. Modern attackers seek long-term persistence and foothold - the ability to survive being caught, password resets, system rebuilds and reboots, privilege revocations, and incident response efforts while maintaining covert access to enterprise identity infrastructure. As unfortunate as it is, Active Directory provides numerous persistence opportunities because identity itself becomes the attack surface.

Chapter 12 explores the offensive methodologies used to maintain access after initial compromise. You will study and examine Golden Tickets, Silver Tickets, Diamond Tickets, Sapphire Tickets, Skeleton Key malware, AdminSDHolder abuse, SIDHistory manipulation, Shadow Credentials, DCShadow, DCSync abuse, malicious Group Policy Objects, machine account abuse, and domain controller persistence. The chapter explains how adversaries' layer multiple persistence mechanisms to create resilient access that often survives complete remedial efforts.

From the defensive perspective, the chapter examines persistence hunting methodologies, directory integrity validation, privileged identity reviews, certificate lifecycle monitoring, replication auditing, authentication anomaly detection, and complete identity recovery procedures. You will also learn why effective remediation requires understanding not only how persistence is created, but also how it propagates throughout enterprise identity infrastructure.

**Chapter Summary**

This chapter examined how adversaries transform temporary administrative access into persistent control of enterprise identity infrastructure. Beginning with Kerberos ticket forgery and extending through network directory and domain object manipulation, certificate abuse, delegation exploitation, replication abuse, and implanted authentication backdoors, you explored how persistence techniques are layered to survive remediation efforts and maintain long-term access. Equally important, the chapter demonstrated defensive methodologies for identifying malicious persistence attempts and techniques, validating directory integrity, auditing privileged accounts and identities, and restoring trust in compromised Active Directory FICAM environments. Understanding persistence is essential because successful incident response requires eliminating every mechanism an attacker has established - not merely removing the initial point or vector of compromise.

**Lessons Learned & Key Takeaways**

- Persistence is often the primary objective after privilege escalation.
- Active Directory offers numerous persistence mechanisms across identities, certificates, delegation, Group Policy, replication, and authentication services.
- Kerberos ticket forgery enables long-term access without continued credential theft.
- AdminSDHolder, SDProp, SIDHistory, and ACL abuse can silently maintain elevated privileges.
- Certificate Services introduces persistence mechanisms that frequently survive password resets, reboots, OS rebuilds, and other eradication efforts.
- Multiple persistence techniques are commonly deployed simultaneously to ensure resilience and covertness.
- Persistence hunting requires comprehensive visibility into authentication, replication, directory modifications, and privileged account activities.
- Effective remediation demands complete eradication of all attacker-established infrastructure and footholds before identity trust can be fully restored and defended.

**Introduction to Next Chapter**

While persistence enables adversaries to maintain covert access for extended periods, defenders must rapidly detect, investigate, eradicate, and triage malicious activities before they expand into enterprise-wide compromise. The next chapter shifts from offensive tradecraft to defensive operations by examining identity-centric detection security engineering, threat hunting, attack containment, forensics investigation, and incident response methodologies designed specifically for Active Directory and FICAM-enabled environments. You will then learn how modern Security and Network Operations Centers (SOC/NOC) detect sophisticated identity attacks, correlate authentication anomalies, investigate attack pathways, and initiate recovery procedures that preserve both operational continuity and organizational trust.

**Editorial Philosophy**

Chapter 12 is where tactical successes become strategic compromises. Readers should leave understanding that attacks such as DCSync and pulling the DIT are not simply credential theft - they represent the collapse of trust within the enterprise identity infrastructure.

**Phase VI - Detection Security Engineering**

## **🔒Module 13 / Chapter 13 v2.0 - Detection Security Engineering**

**Module 13 Operational Intent & Knowledge Ownership Matrix**

**Module Title**

**Detection Security Engineering**

**Operational Intent**

Establish how defenders engineer, validate, tune, operationalize, and continuously improve identity-centric detections across Active Directory, FICAM-aligned identity systems, hybrid identity environments, cloud identity platforms, certificate services, federation services, endpoints, networks, and mission security operations.

**Educational Objectives**

Upon completion of this module, the reader should understand that identity detection is an engineering discipline, not a collection of isolated alerts. The reader will be able to explain how defenders translate identity attack behaviors into telemetry requirements, detection hypotheses, SIEM correlation logic, identity analytics, threat hunting procedures, deception strategies, attack path disruption, alert triage, containment triggers, purple team validation, and continuous monitoring workflows.

This module teaches defenders how to detect and investigate the identity attack lifecycle introduced across Modules 6 through 12, including passive exposure, active enumeration, initial identity acquisition, credential acquisition, identity authority expansion, enterprise identity mobility, persistence, and domain dominance. It also teaches how detection engineering supports mission assurance by giving SOC analysts, identity engineers, ISSOs, ISSMs, incident responders, and mission defenders the ability to identify identity compromise before trust collapses.

**Identity Lifecycle Phase - Identity Detection, Monitoring, and Threat Hunting**

This module owns the detection, monitoring, and threat hunting phase of the identity lifecycle. It explains how identity systems are instrumented, monitored, baselined, analyzed, hunted, and validated so defenders can identify suspicious authentication, authorization, credential, federation, certificate, replication, privilege, and movement behaviors.

This module does not own full recovery execution, forest recovery, KRBTGT reset execution, AD CS recovery execution, long-term remediation planning, operational lessons learned, or future identity modernization. Those activities are intentionally deferred to Modules 14, 15, and 16. The purpose of Module 13 is to teach how identity-centric attacks are detected, investigated, enriched, prioritized, and disrupted before the curriculum advances into identity recovery and domain trust restoration in Module 14.

**Adversary Lifecycle Phase - Detection, Threat Hunting, and Continuous Monitoring**

This module shifts from adversary operations into defender operations. It consumes the adversary lifecycle from Modules 6 through 12 and teaches how each phase generates observable identity, endpoint, directory, network, certificate, federation, cloud, and behavioral signals.

This module does not reteach reconnaissance, enumeration, initial identity acquisition, credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, or domain dominance as offensive disciplines. Instead, it teaches how defenders detect, hunt, validate, correlate, and disrupt those behaviors. Incident response execution, full recovery, and post-compromise trust restoration are intentionally deferred to Module 14.

**Primary Knowledge Ownership**

**Module 13 exclusively owns:**

- **Identity Detection Engineering.** The discipline of converting identity attack behaviors, architectural risks, and mission threats into engineered detections, telemetry requirements, correlation logic, alert workflows, validation plans, and continuous improvement processes.
- **Threat-Informed Detection.** The process of using adversary behavior, MITRE ATT&CK mapping, prior assessment findings, identity attack paths, and mission risk to prioritize detection development.
- **Identity Telemetry Architecture.** The identification of required telemetry sources across Domain Controllers, endpoints, Kerberos, NTLM, LDAP, DNS, AD CS, AD FS, Microsoft Entra ID, federation services, cloud identity platforms, EDR, network sensors, SIEM platforms, and identity security tools.
- **Windows and Active Directory Logging Strategy.** The defensive use of Security logs, Directory Services logs, DNS logs, AD CS logs, PowerShell logs, Sysmon, authentication logs, privileged access logs, and directory change events as identity detection sources.
- **Kerberos Detection Engineering.** Detection strategy for Kerberos anomalies, ticket abuse indicators, unusual service ticket requests, AS-REP Roasting indicators, Kerberoasting indicators, ticket forgery indicators, Golden Ticket indicators, Silver Ticket indicators, and suspicious authentication patterns.
- **NTLM Detection Engineering.** Detection strategy for NTLM usage, NTLM relay risk, legacy authentication exposure, unexpected NTLM authentication paths, authentication coercion indicators, and NTLM reduction validation.
- **LDAP and Directory Reconnaissance Detection.** Detection strategy for active enumeration, unusual LDAP query patterns, BloodHound-style collection indicators, trust discovery, group discovery, ACL discovery, SPN enumeration, and abnormal directory access.
- **Initial Identity Acquisition Detection.** Detection strategy for password spraying, credential stuffing, phishing-related sign-in anomalies, MFA fatigue indicators, OAuth consent abuse, external remote service abuse, federation entry anomalies, Microsoft Entra ID sign-in anomalies, and hybrid identity foothold indicators.
- **Credential Acquisition Detection.** Detection strategy for AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, LSASS access, credential dumping indicators, SAM access, cached credential exposure, service account secret exposure, token misuse, cloud token anomalies, and credential lifecycle failures.
- **Identity Authority Expansion Detection.** Detection strategy for ACL changes, DACL changes, ACE abuse indicators, privileged group changes, delegation changes, RBCD exposure, Shadow Credentials, AdminSDHolder changes, SIDHistory anomalies, AD CS template abuse, AD CS relay-chain indicators, coercion-assisted escalation indicators, and Microsoft Entra administrative role abuse.
- **Enterprise Identity Mobility Detection.** Detection strategy for Pass-the-Hash, Pass-the-Ticket, Overpass-the-Hash, NTLM Relay, PrinterBug / MS-RPRN activity, SMB movement, PsExec-style execution, WinRM abuse, WMI abuse, DCOM activity, RDP anomalies, PowerShell Remoting abuse, remote service creation, scheduled task movement, cross-domain traversal, and cross-forest authentication anomalies.
- **Persistence and Domain Dominance Detection.** Detection strategy for NTDS.dit exposure, DCSync, DCShadow, KRBTGT compromise, Golden Ticket activity, Silver Ticket activity, Diamond Ticket activity, Sapphire Ticket activity, Skeleton Key indicators, AdminSDHolder persistence, SIDHistory persistence, Shadow Credentials, malicious Group Policy, certificate persistence, rogue certificate authorities, rogue federation trusts, service account persistence, Domain Controller persistence, and forest-level compromise indicators.
- **AD CS Detection Engineering.** Detection strategy for certificate authority abuse, certificate template modification, risky enrollment behavior, authentication-capable template exposure, unusual certificate issuance, certificate mapping anomalies, private key risk, and certificate-based persistence indicators.
- **Federation Detection Engineering.** Detection strategy for AD FS anomalies, token-signing certificate issues, claims rule changes, relying party trust changes, federation metadata anomalies, rogue federation relationships, and suspicious federated authentication behavior.
- **Microsoft Entra ID Detection Engineering.** Detection strategy for risky sign-ins, impossible travel, token anomalies, OAuth consent abuse, administrative role changes, Conditional Access gaps, suspicious application activity, hybrid identity anomalies, privileged cloud role abuse, and cloud identity persistence indicators.
- **Hybrid Identity Detection.** Detection strategy for Entra Connect risk, synchronization anomalies, hybrid authentication anomalies, cloud-to-on-premises identity pathways, federated identity bridges, and hybrid privilege relationships.
- **Identity Threat Hunting.** The proactive search for identity compromise using hypotheses, baselines, anomaly analysis, graph relationships, attack path knowledge, privileged account review, service account review, authentication analytics, and historical telemetry.
- **Continuous Monitoring.** The ongoing measurement of identity activity, configuration drift, privilege changes, authentication behavior, certificate activity, federation changes, trust relationships, and Tier 0 integrity.
- **Detection Lifecycle Management.** The process of designing, testing, tuning, documenting, approving, deploying, maintaining, and retiring identity detections.
- **Detection-as-Code Concepts.** The controlled development of detection logic, versioned queries, Sigma rules, KQL queries, Splunk searches, analytic rules, and validation artifacts without making this module a tool-specific query catalog.
- **Alert Triage and Enrichment.** The process of enriching alerts with identity context, asset criticality, privilege level, mission relevance, historical behavior, attack path proximity, user risk, device risk, and potential blast radius.
- **Identity Analytics and UEBA.** The use of behavioral baselines, User and Entity Behavior Analytics (UEBA), anomaly scoring, peer grouping, risk scoring, and identity activity modeling to identify suspicious behavior.
- **Deception and Honeytokens.** The defensive use of honey accounts, honey credentials, honey SPNs, honey services, honey certificates, decoy objects, and deception telemetry to detect adversary behavior.
- **Attack Path Disruption.** The defensive use of attack path analysis to identify where detections, controls, containment actions, and remediation can interrupt identity compromise.
- **Purple Team Validation.** The use of authorized adversary emulation, detection validation, tabletop testing, simulated attack paths, controlled telemetry generation, and collaborative defender feedback to validate detection coverage.
- **AI-Assisted Detection Workflows.** The use of AI-assisted alert enrichment, anomaly clustering, triage support, query generation assistance, analyst decision support, report drafting, and pattern discovery while maintaining human validation and governance.
- **Detection Metrics and Maturity.** The use of coverage, fidelity, false positive rate, false negative risk, mean time to detect, mean time to triage, detection gaps, telemetry gaps, ATT&CK coverage, and mission-impact alignment to measure detection maturity.
- **Containment Triggering Concepts.** The identification of conditions that should trigger containment consideration, such as suspected DCSync, KRBTGT compromise, Golden Ticket activity, Domain Controller compromise, rogue certificates, federation compromise, or Tier 0 abuse, while recovery execution remains owned by Module 14.
- **Detection Engineering Boundaries and Ethics.** The requirement that detection validation, purple team activity, telemetry collection, alert enrichment, and monitoring remain authorized, scoped, privacy-aware, deconflicted, documented, and aligned with Rules of Engagement (RoE), mission requirements, legal boundaries, and operational safety.

**Knowledge Dependencies**

**This module assumes the reader already understands:**

- Identity as the foundational security boundary.
- Identity as the primary operational battlefield.
- Identity as the primary attack surface.
- The relationship among identity, credentials, authentication, authorization, accounting, access control, and trust.
- FICAM and ICAM as federal identity governance models.
- Identity governance, credential governance, access governance, federation, assurance, and mission assurance.
- Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS), directory services, Domain Controllers, DNS, LDAP, Kerberos, NTLM, PKI, AD CS, AD FS, Microsoft Entra ID, federation, trusts, Group Policy, replication, and hybrid identity at an architectural level.
- Assessment methodology, Rules of Engagement (RoE), authorization boundaries, scope, evidence handling, risk reporting, and operational preparation.
- Identity security engineering, administrative tiering, privileged access governance, baseline validation, Group Policy security, PKI governance, AD CS security, identity hygiene, authentication hardening, credential hygiene, service account governance, and configuration drift.
- Passive identity reconnaissance, OSINT, DNS analysis, certificate transparency, public repository exposure, public cloud identity indicators, federation metadata discovery, breach exposure awareness, and external attack surface management.
- Active identity enumeration, user discovery, group discovery, computer discovery, SPN discovery, GPO discovery, ACL discovery, trust discovery, certificate template discovery, session discovery, share discovery, service account discovery, and identity graph analysis.
- Initial identity acquisition, password spraying, credential stuffing, phishing, OAuth consent abuse, MFA fatigue, external remote services, AD FS entry points, Microsoft Entra ID exposure, and hybrid identity footholds at a conceptual level.
- Credential acquisition, credentials as identity artifacts, AS-REP Roasting, Kerberoasting, LSASS exposure, cached credential exposure, SAM exposure, Kerberos ticket material, token theft, service account secrets, managed service account exposure, cloud tokens, and certificate-backed credential material at a conceptual level.
- Identity authority expansion, authorization abuse, ACL abuse, delegation abuse, AD CS escalation paths, PetitPotam, coercion-assisted escalation, relay chains, Microsoft Entra administrative role abuse, shadow administrators, and privileged access governance failures at a conceptual level.
- Enterprise identity mobility, Pass-the-Hash, Pass-the-Ticket, Overpass-the-Hash, NTLM Relay, PrinterBug / MS-RPRN, SMB, PsExec, WinRM, WMI, DCOM, RDP, PowerShell Remoting, remote service creation, scheduled task movement, trust traversal, and cross-forest authentication at a conceptual level.
- Persistence and domain dominance, NTDS.dit exposure, DCSync, DCShadow, KRBTGT compromise, Golden Ticket, Silver Ticket, Diamond Ticket, Sapphire Ticket, Skeleton Key, AdminSDHolder persistence, SIDHistory persistence, certificate persistence, rogue federation trusts, Domain Controller persistence, and forest-level compromise at a conceptual level.

This module does not assume prior hands-on experience building SIEM detections, writing Sigma rules, developing KQL or Splunk searches, tuning Microsoft Defender for Identity, deploying UEBA analytics, operating deception systems, conducting purple team validation, building detection-as-code pipelines, or engineering AI-assisted detection workflows.

Module 13 is the detection engineering dependency for the curriculum because every later defender module examines identity either as a signal source, alerting problem, hunting domain, containment trigger, recovery input, operational lesson, or future analytics challenge.

**Federal / DoD Operational Perspective**

Module 13 examines identity detection engineering as a mission defense function across federal civilian agencies, Defense Agencies, Combatant Commands, Intelligence Community elements, military departments, and mission-partner environments.

The operational thesis is that identity compromise cannot be defended if it cannot be seen. Federal and defense environments depend upon identity telemetry to detect misuse of valid accounts, credential attacks, privilege abuse, Kerberos anomalies, certificate abuse, federation compromise, lateral movement, persistence, and domain dominance. Detection failures may allow an adversary to remain trusted by the enterprise identity infrastructure long after the initial compromise occurred.

In federal and defense environments, identity detection engineering affects SOC operations, CSSP visibility, incident triage, mission owner notification, ISSO and ISSM coordination, authorizing official risk decisions, commander awareness, evidence preservation, operational continuity, and escalation to recovery operations. Missing telemetry, poor correlation, weak alert tuning, inadequate threat hunting, or ignored identity alerts can allow tactical compromise to become strategic mission risk.

This module frames detection engineering as a mission assurance function because commanders, system owners, ISSOs, ISSMs, authorizing officials, security engineers, identity administrators, SOC analysts, CSSPs, incident responders, and mission defenders require reliable identity detection to determine when trust is being abused, degraded, or lost.

**Transition**

Having established how defenders detect, hunt, validate, enrich, and disrupt identity-centric attacks, the reader is now prepared to examine how agencies, Commands, and mission partners respond when identity trust has been compromised. Module 14 transitions from detection security engineering into identity recovery and domain trust restoration by introducing identity incident response, containment strategy, KRBTGT reset planning, forest recovery, AD CS recovery, certificate revocation, federation trust repair, password rotation, service account rotation, privileged account recertification, Tier 0 reconstitution, Domain Controller validation, trust repair, post-compromise hardening, and restoration of confidence in enterprise identity infrastructure.

**Phase VII - Identity Recovery & Domain Trust Restoration**

## **🔒Module 14 / Chapter 14 v2.0 - Identity Recovery and Domain Trust Restoration**

**Module 14 Operational Intent & Knowledge Ownership Matrix**

**Module Title**

**Identity Recovery and Domain Trust Restoration**

**Operational Intent**

Establish how agencies, Commands, mission partners, and enterprise defenders restore confidence in identity infrastructure after compromise by containing identity abuse, validating trust boundaries, eliminating persistence, recovering Active Directory, repairing certificate and federation trust, rotating compromised credentials, reconstituting Tier 0, and proving that authentication and authorization can be trusted again.

**Educational Objectives**

Upon completion of this module, the reader should understand that identity recovery is not merely system restoration. The reader will be able to explain how defenders recover from identity compromise by validating the scope of compromise, preserving evidence, containing identity abuse, isolating Tier 0 assets, planning KRBTGT reset activity, recovering domain controllers, restoring forest integrity, remediating AD CS compromise, revoking certificates, repairing federation trust, rotating passwords, rotating service accounts, revalidating privileged access, restoring administrative boundaries, and verifying that enterprise trust has been reestablished.

This module teaches recovery as a confidence-restoration discipline. It shows that after DCSync, Golden Ticket activity, NTDS.dit exposure, KRBTGT compromise, rogue certificates, federation abuse, Domain Controller compromise, or forest-level persistence, defenders must do more than remove malware or reset a few passwords. They must prove that identity infrastructure can once again make reliable authentication, authorization, credential, certificate, federation, and administrative trust decisions.

**Identity Lifecycle Phase - Identity Recovery, Reconstitution, and Trust Restoration**

This module owns the recovery and trust-restoration phase of the identity lifecycle. It explains how compromised identity infrastructure is contained, validated, cleaned, rebuilt, reconfigured, recredentialed, recertified, reauthorized, monitored, and returned to trusted operation.

This module does not own initial detection engineering, ongoing threat hunting, operational field lessons, or future modernization strategy. Detection engineering is owned by Module 13. Operational lessons are owned by Module 15. Future identity architectures are owned by Module 16. The purpose of Module 14 is to teach how defenders move from "we detected identity compromise" to "we have restored confidence in identity trust."

**Adversary Lifecycle Phase - Recovery, Reconstitution, and Trust Revalidation**

This module shifts fully into post-compromise defender operations. It consumes the adversary lifecycle from Modules 6 through 12 and the detection lifecycle from Module 13, then teaches how defenders contain compromise, remove persistence, rotate identity material, repair trust relationships, validate integrity, and restore operational confidence.

This module does not reteach reconnaissance, enumeration, initial identity acquisition, credential acquisition, privilege escalation, lateral movement, persistence, or domain dominance as offensive disciplines. It also does not reteach detection engineering as a primary topic. Instead, it teaches how defenders recover after those behaviors have placed identity trust at risk.

**Primary Knowledge Ownership**

**Module 14 exclusively owns:**

- **Identity Recovery.** The process of restoring reliable authentication, authorization, credential integrity, administrative control, certificate trust, federation trust, and operational identity assurance after compromise.
- **Domain Trust Restoration.** The process of restoring confidence in Active Directory domains, forests, trusts, Domain Controllers, Kerberos, replication, Group Policy, certificate services, privileged access, and federation relationships after identity compromise.
- **Identity Incident Response.** The response discipline focused specifically on compromised identities, credentials, accounts, tokens, tickets, certificates, privileged access, domain trust, federation trust, and identity infrastructure.
- **Post-Compromise Scope Validation.** The process of determining which identities, credentials, groups, permissions, certificates, service accounts, Domain Controllers, trusts, federation relationships, and cloud identity components were affected.
- **Containment Strategy for Identity Compromise.** The planning and execution logic required to prevent ongoing abuse of compromised identities, credential material, privileged sessions, federation trust, certificate trust, or Domain Controller control.
- **Tier 0 Isolation.** The protection, containment, validation, and reconstitution of Domain Controllers, certificate authorities, federation infrastructure, privileged identity systems, administrative workstations, Entra Connect servers, and other Tier 0 assets.
- **Evidence Preservation for Identity Recovery.** The preservation of identity logs, Domain Controller artifacts, replication evidence, certificate issuance data, federation configuration, authentication records, endpoint telemetry, and administrative change records needed to support recovery decisions.
- **Recovery Decision Points.** The determination of whether to contain, rotate, rebuild, restore, isolate, revoke, demote, reissue, reconfigure, or reconstitute identity infrastructure based on compromise severity.
- **KRBTGT Reset Planning.** The conceptual ownership of KRBTGT reset planning, sequencing, validation, operational risk, service impact, ticket invalidation, and trust restoration following suspected Kerberos trust compromise.
- **KRBTGT Double Reset Concepts.** The recovery concept that KRBTGT reset activity may require staged resets and careful validation when forged Kerberos ticket risk exists.
- **Golden Ticket Recovery.** The recovery logic required when forged Ticket Granting Tickets may exist, including trust reset planning, ticket invalidation, telemetry validation, and post-reset monitoring.
- **DCSync Recovery.** The recovery logic required when replication privileges were abused or sensitive credential material may have been obtained from Domain Controllers.
- **NTDS.dit Exposure Recovery.** The recovery implications of NTDS.dit exposure, including enterprise credential compromise assumptions, password rotation strategy, privileged account review, service account rotation, and domain trust validation.
- **Domain Controller Recovery.** The recovery, validation, rebuild, demotion, replacement, or reconstitution logic associated with compromised, suspicious, corrupted, or untrusted Domain Controllers.
- **Forest Recovery.** The conceptual ownership of forest-level recovery planning, including forest trust validation, Domain Controller integrity, replication integrity, privileged access integrity, and restoration of forest-wide confidence.
- **Replication Integrity Validation.** The validation of replication health, replication metadata, suspicious replication permissions, unauthorized replication behavior, and domain controller trust after compromise.
- **FSMO Role Recovery Considerations.** The recovery implications of compromised or unavailable FSMO role holders, including role validation, transfer, seizure considerations, and operational continuity.
- **Active Directory Object Integrity Review.** The review of users, groups, computers, OUs, ACLs, DACLs, ACEs, AdminSDHolder, SIDHistory, delegation, RBCD, Shadow Credentials, service accounts, GPOs, and privileged objects for attacker-created persistence or unauthorized changes.
- **AdminSDHolder and SDProp Recovery.** The restoration and validation of AdminSDHolder permissions and SDProp behavior after privileged object persistence or authorization abuse.
- **SIDHistory Recovery.** The validation and removal of unauthorized SIDHistory values or trust-related authorization artifacts introduced during compromise.
- **Shadow Credential Recovery.** The identification and removal of unauthorized key trust, alternate credential, certificate-backed, or identity-binding material used for persistence.
- **Delegation Recovery.** The review and remediation of unconstrained delegation, constrained delegation, RBCD, service delegation, and delegation paths modified or abused during compromise.
- **Group Policy Recovery.** The review, restoration, cleanup, and validation of GPOs, GPO links, security filtering, startup scripts, logon scripts, scheduled tasks, policy-based persistence, and SYSVOL integrity.
- **AD CS Recovery.** The recovery and validation of enterprise certificate authorities, certificate templates, enrollment permissions, issuance policies, CA configuration, private keys, certificate mappings, and certificate-based authentication controls.
- **Certificate Revocation.** The recovery process of identifying, revoking, replacing, and monitoring certificates that may have been abused, improperly issued, stolen, or used for persistence.
- **Certificate Template Revalidation.** The post-compromise review of certificate templates, enrollment rights, authentication-capable templates, issuance requirements, manager approval settings, and template permissions.
- **Rogue Certificate Authority Review.** The identification and removal of unauthorized, compromised, untrusted, or attacker-controlled certificate authorities and certificate trust anchors.
- **Federation Trust Recovery.** The recovery of AD FS, SAML, OpenID Connect, OAuth, token-signing certificates, relying party trusts, claims rules, identity provider trust, federation metadata, and federated authentication pathways after compromise.
- **Rogue Federation Trust Removal.** The identification and removal of unauthorized or attacker-controlled federation relationships, claims rules, token-signing material, relying party trusts, and trust objects.
- **Cloud and Hybrid Identity Recovery.** The recovery of Microsoft Entra ID, Entra Connect, synchronization relationships, cloud administrative roles, Conditional Access policy, OAuth applications, refresh tokens, PRTs, application secrets, service principals, and hybrid identity trust after compromise.
- **Password Rotation Strategy.** The prioritization, sequencing, validation, and operational management of password resets across privileged accounts, standard users, local accounts, emergency accounts, service accounts, application accounts, and mission-critical identities.
- **Service Account Rotation.** The recovery planning required for rotating service account credentials without breaking mission systems, applications, scheduled tasks, scripts, databases, integrations, or operational dependencies.
- **Token Revocation and Session Invalidation.** The recovery of cloud and hybrid identity control by revoking refresh tokens, invalidating sessions, removing unauthorized devices, reviewing application consent, and revalidating active sessions.
- **Privileged Account Recertification.** The review and reapproval of privileged accounts, privileged groups, administrative roles, emergency access, break-glass accounts, delegated administrators, Entra roles, and Tier 0 personnel access.
- **Identity Verification and Validation.** The process of verifying that identities, credentials, access rights, certificates, federation relationships, and administrative authorities are legitimate after compromise.
- **Identity Reverification and Revalidation.** The post-compromise process of revalidating identity proofing, credential legitimacy, role assignment, entitlement accuracy, privileged access need, and authorization boundaries.
- **Tier 0 Reconstitution.** The controlled restoration of Tier 0 administrative capability, including Domain Controllers, certificate authorities, federation infrastructure, privileged workstations, administrative accounts, privileged access workflows, and monitoring coverage.
- **Post-Incident Hardening.** The implementation of hardening actions after recovery including authentication hardening, privilege reduction, AD CS remediation, federation cleanup, service account governance, GPO cleanup, trust reduction, monitoring improvements, and baseline revalidation.
- **Recovery Validation and Confidence Building.** The process of demonstrating to technical teams, mission owners, authorizing officials, commanders, SOC teams, and stakeholders that identity infrastructure has been cleaned, re-secured, monitored, and returned to trusted operation.
- **Return-to-Operations Criteria.** The definition of evidence-based criteria for returning identity infrastructure to normal operations after compromise.
- **Recovery Boundaries and Ethics.** The requirement that identity recovery actions remain authorized, coordinated, documented, evidence-preserving, mission-aware, privacy-aware, and aligned with operational priorities, legal requirements, and continuity of operations.

- Identity authority expansion, authorization abuse, ACL abuse, delegation abuse, AD CS escalation paths, PetitPotam, coercion-assisted escalation, relay chains, Microsoft Entra administrative role abuse, shadow administrators, and privileged access governance failures at a conceptual level.
- Enterprise identity mobility, Pass-the-Hash, Pass-the-Ticket, Overpass-the-Hash, NTLM Relay, PrinterBug / MS-RPRN, SMB, PsExec, WinRM, WMI, DCOM, RDP, PowerShell Remoting, remote service creation, scheduled task movement, trust traversal, and cross-forest authentication at a conceptual level.

**Defensive Ownership**

This module owns identity recovery and domain trust restoration from the defender's perspective.

**Module 14 owns:**

- **Identity incident response**
  - Identity incident response is the structured operational process of identifying, containing, and remediating compromised digital signatures to stop attackers from moving laterally or accessing sensitive systems.
- **Identity compromise containment**
  - Identity compromise containment is the rapid isolation and restriction of breached user accounts, credentials, or access rights to prevent unauthorized lateral movement, data theft, and further damage to a network.
- **Identity recovery planning**
  - Identity recovery planning is the structured strategy and set of established procedures used to securely and rapidly restore an organization's compromised or failed AD infrastructure to a clean, functional state, ensuring business continuity and preventing prolonged downtime.
- **Domain trust restoration**
  - Domain trust restoration is the process of repairing or resetting the secure communication channel between a computer and a domain controller to resolve authentication errors (such as "the trust relationship failed") and allows users to log in with their network credentials.
- **Forest recovery concepts**
  - Forest recovery concepts refer to the strategies, phases, and technical procedures required to safely restore an entire Active Directory infrastructure to a known healthy state following a catastrophic, domain-wide failure or ransomware attack.
- **Domain Controller recovery concepts**
  - Domain Controller recovery concepts focus on the specific technical procedures, such as non-authoritative or authoritative restores, used to rebuild or repair an individual domain controller from a backup after hardware failure or database corruption.
- **Domain Controller validation**
  - Domain Controller validation is the process of verifying that a restored or newly built domain controller is functioning correctly, free of malware, and operating securely.
- **Replication integrity validation**
  - Replication integrity validation involves checking that Active Directory database updates are synchronizing successfully and without errors across all surviving domain controllers.
- **FSMO role recovery considerations**
  - FSMO recovery considerations involve determining whether to seize or gracefully transfer critical, centralized Flexible Single Master Operation roles when the original holder is offline or compromised.
- **KRBTGT reset planning**
  - KRBTGT reset planning is the strategic scheduling and coordination required to change the password of the service account that signs all Kerberos tickets, minimizing disruption to user logins.
- **KRBTGT double reset concepts**
  - KRBTGT double reset concepts refer to the mandatory practice of changing the KRBTGT password twice in succession to invalidate both current and previous password hashes, effectively destroying existing rogue Kerberos tickets.
- **Golden Ticket recovery**
  - Golden Ticket recovery is the remediation process of purging persistent unauthorized administrative access gained by attackers who forged long-term Kerberos Ticket Granting Tickets.
- **DCSync recovery**
  - DCSync recovery focuses on auditing and removing unauthorized "Get Changes All" replication permissions that allow attackers to impersonate a domain controller and dump password hashes remotely.
- **NTDS.dit exposure recovery**
  - NTDS.dit exposure recovery is the emergency response plan executed when the main Active Directory database file is stolen, requiring a total reset of all domain secrets because all passwords must be assumed compromised.
- **DCShadow recovery considerations**
  - DCShadow recovery considerations involve identifying and purging rogue, temporary domain controllers injected into the network by attackers to register malicious directory changes.
- **Tier 0 isolation**
  - Tier 0 isolation is the immediate operational containment strategy of cutting off network connectivity between the highest-privilege identity infrastructure and untrusted or compromised lower-tier networks.
- **Tier 0 reconstitution**
  - Tier 0 reconstitution is the systematic process of rebuilding, patching, and hardening the core identity framework from a known clean state after a major incident.
- **Privileged account recertification**
  - Privileged account recertification is the formal audit process of verifying that every high-privileged user still requires their elevated access rights.
- **Privileged group review**
  - Privileged group review is the manual inspection of administrative groups to detect and remove unauthorized or nested accounts added by an attacker.
- **AdminSDHolder recovery**
  - AdminSDHolder recovery is the process of restoring the default permissions of the AdminSDHolder object to stop malicious, inherited access templates from propagating to protected administrative accounts.
- **SDProp validation**
  - SDProp validation is the verification that the Security Descriptor Propagator process is successfully running and enforcing secure permission templates on all protected objects.
- **SIDHistory recovery**
  - SIDHistory recovery involves auditing and clearing populated SID History attributes to prevent attackers from using old security identifiers to maintain stealthy, cross-domain administrative access.
- **Shadow credential recovery**
  - Shadow credential recovery is the detection and removal of unauthorized public key certificates injected into user object attributes that allow passwordless authentication.
- **Delegation recovery**
  - Delegation recovery is the process of auditing classic Kerberos delegation configurations to revoke unconstrained or constrained privileges that could lead to lateral movement.
- **Resource-Based Conditional Access (RBCD) recovery**
  - RBCD recovery involves auditing and clearing Resource-Based Constrained Delegation attributes configured on target computer objects to block unauthorized impersonation.
- **ACL / DACL / ACE cleanup after compromise**
  - ACL / DACL / ACE Cleanup After Compromise is the comprehensive process of scrubbing security descriptors to remove hidden explicit permissions, backdoor entries, or malicious access control modifications.
- **Group Policy recovery**
  - Group Policy recovery is the process of auditing, repairing, or restoring Group Policy Objects from clean backups to eliminate malicious scripts, rogue registry modifications, or unauthorized software deployments. \[[1](https://www.cayosoft.com/active-directory-management-tools/active-directory-security/)\].
- **SYSVOL integrity validation**
  - Verification that the SYSVOL share - the replicated folder storing Group Policy Objects (GPOs), logon scripts, and domain configurations - has not been tampered with, ensuring adversaries have not injected malicious scripts or modified security policies to establish persistence or escalate privileges.
- **Malicious GPO cleanup**
  - **Attacker:** The deliberate removal or obfuscation of adversary-created Group Policy Objects (GPOs) to erase persistence artifacts and evidence, evade security detections, and complicate incident response efforts. Attackers may delete rogue GPOs after achieving objectives, modify GPO metadata to mask attribution, or corrupt legitimate policies to degrade forensic recovery efforts.
  - **Defender:** The systematic identification, quarantine, and removal of unauthorized Group Policy Objects (GPOs), malicious scripts, registry modifications, or security policy changes deployed through SYSVOL. Defenders sever replication, revoke delegated permissions, eliminate persistence artifacts and evidence, and restore verified baselines to prevent re-compromise or re-entry into the domain network, restoring policy integrity domain-wide,
- **Startup and logon script cleanup**
  - **Attacker:** The deliberate removal, modification, or obfuscation of malicious startup and logon scripts to erase persistence artifacts and evidence, evade detection, and complicate forensics analysis investigations. Attackers may delete scripts to "cover their tracks," deleting software remnants that may leave behind their footprint all done under the premise of misleading incident responders and security teams.
  - **Defender:** The systematic identification, removal, and remediation of unauthorized startup and logon scripts deployed through Group Policy, local policy, registry run keys, or scheduled tasks. Defenders audit script execution paths, validate script integrity against known-good baselines, remove malicious entries, and restore legitimate configurations to eliminate persistence mechanisms and prevent re-compromise across endpoints and domain-joined systems.
- **Scheduled task persistence cleanup**
  - **Attacker:** The deliberate removal, modification, or obfuscation of malicious scheduled tasks to erase persistence artifacts, evade detection, and complicate forensic analysis. Attackers may delete rogue tasks after establishing alternate persistence, alter task metadata to mimic legitimate system processes, modify execution triggers to appear benign, or corrupt existing tasks to degrade operational continuity and mislead incident responders.
  - **Defenders:** The systematic identification, disablement, and removal of unauthorized scheduled tasks deployed through the Task Scheduler service, Group Policy, WMI event subscriptions, or remote management interfaces. Defenders audit task execution history, validate task binaries and arguments against known-good baselines, sever WMI event consumer bindings, remove malicious entries from the Task Scheduler database and registry, and restore legitimate task configurations to eliminate persistence mechanisms and prevent re-compromise across endpoints and domain-joined systems.
- **Windows Management Instrumentation (WMI) persistence cleanup**
  - **Attacker:** The deliberate removal, modification, or obfuscation of malicious WMI event subscriptions to erase persistence artifacts, evade detection, and complicate forensic analysis. Attackers may delete rogue event filters, consumers, and bindings after establishing alternate persistence, alter WMI repository objects to mimic legitimate system telemetry, or corrupt existing subscriptions to degrade operational continuity and mislead incident responders.
    - **Accomplished via:** wmic, PowerShell Get-WmiObject/Remove-WmiObject, mofcomp, or direct manipulation of the WMI repository (C:\\Windows\\System32\\wbem\\Repository).
  - **Defender:** The systematic identification, disablement, and removal of unauthorized WMI event filters, consumers, and bindings deployed for persistent command execution or data exfiltration. Defenders audit WMI subscription namespaces (root\\subscription, root\\default), validate event consumer binaries and command lines against known-good baselines, remove malicious filter-to-consumer bindings, purge rogue repository objects, and restore legitimate WMI configurations to eliminate persistence mechanisms and prevent re-compromise across endpoints and domain-joined systems.
    - **Accomplished via:** Sysinternals Autoruns, PowerShell Get-CimInstance/Remove-CimInstance, WMIGen, Kansa, or manual MOF file inspection and removal.
- **Service persistence cleanup**
  - **Attacker:** The deliberate removal, modification, or obfuscation of malicious Windows services to erase persistence artifacts, evade detection, and complicate forensic analysis. Attackers may delete rogue services after establishing alternate persistence, alter service binaries or registry entries to mimic legitimate system processes, modify service configurations to appear benign, or corrupt existing services to degrade operational continuity and mislead incident responders.
    - **Accomplished via:** sc.exe, PowerShell New-Service/Set-Service, registry manipulation (HKLM\\SYSTEM\\CurrentControlSet\\Services), or direct service control manager API calls.
  - **Defender:** The systematic identification, disablement, and removal of unauthorized Windows services deployed for persistent command execution, privilege escalation, or lateral movement. Defenders audit service binaries, image paths, and registry configurations against known-good baselines, validate digital signatures, remove malicious service entries from the Service Control Manager database and registry, terminate associated processes, and restore legitimate service configurations to eliminate persistence mechanisms and prevent re-compromise across endpoints and domain-joined systems.
    - **Accomplished via:** Sysinternals Autoruns, sc.exe query/delete, PowerShell Get-Service/Remove-Service, services.msc, or forensic analysis with ChkAutorun and OSQuery.
- **Service account rotation**
  - The periodic replacement of credentials (passwords or keys) associated with non-interactive service accounts - including managed service accounts (MSAs), group managed service accounts (gMSAs), virtual service accounts, and traditional service accounts - to limit the window of exposure if credentials are compromised, reduce persistent access opportunities for adversaries, and enforce the principle of least privilege across automated processes, applications, and infrastructure services.
- **Password rotation strategy**
  - A planned approach for systematically changing credentials across user accounts, service accounts, and privileged identities at defined intervals or in response to compromise indicators, minimizing the window of exposure for stolen credentials.
- **Emergency account review**
  - Post-incident audit of emergency access accounts to verify they have not been compromised, misused, or left in an overprivileged state after incident response activities conclude.
- **Break-glass account review**
  - Inspection of break-glass (firecall) administrative accounts to confirm they remain properly secured, unused except in genuine emergencies, and have not been subverted by adversaries during an incident.
- **AD CS recovery**
  - Restoration of Active Directory Certificate Services (AD CS) infrastructure - including CA databases, private keys, certificate templates, and CRL distribution points - following compromise, corruption, or operational failure.
- **Certificate template revalidation**
  - Review and re-authorization of Active Directory certificate templates to ensure they enforce appropriate security settings, restrict enrollment permissions, and have not been maliciously modified to enable privilege escalation (e.g., ESC1-ESC15).
- **Rogue Certificate Authority (CA) review**
  - Detection and elimination of unauthorized or attacker-installed certificate authorities within the enterprise PKI hierarchy. Rogue CAs allow adversaries to forge trusted credentials, intercept encrypted communications, and establish persistent, undetectable access by issuing certificates that appear legitimate to domain-joined systems and applications.
- **Certificate Revocation**
  - The formal invalidation of issued certificates prior to their natural expiration, published via Certificate Revocation Lists (CRLs) or Online Certificate Status Protocol (OCSP) responders, to prevent their continued use by compromised entities.
- **Certificate trust repair**
  - Restoration of correct certificate trust chains, root CA stores, and CTL (Certificate Trust List) configurations to eliminate attacker-introduced trust anchors and reestablish legitimate cryptographic verification.
- **Rogue Federation Trust removal**
  - Detection and elimination of unauthorized federation trusts established by adversaries to maintain persistent cross-domain or cross-tenant access to enterprise resources
- **Federation Trust recovery**
  - Reconstruction of legitimate trust relationships between identity providers and relying parties after adversary tampering, metadata poisoning, or unauthorized federation partner additions.
- **Token-signing certificate review**
  - Inspection of certificates used to sign security tokens in federation and SSO environments to detect unauthorized replacements, weak cryptographic configurations, or compromise indicators.
- **Claims rule review**
  - Audit of attribute release and transformation rules in federation identity providers to identify malicious modifications that could enable unauthorized access, privilege escalation, or data exfiltration
- **Microsoft Entra ID recovery considerations**
  - Planning and execution for restoring Entra ID tenant integrity after compromise, including directory object recovery, policy reconstruction, and verification that cloud-native identities have not been persistently subverted.
- **Relying Party (RP) trust review**
  - Inspection of configured relying party trusts to verify that only legitimate applications and services receive authentication tokens, and that no attacker-controlled endpoints have been added.
- **Entra Connect recovery considerations**
  - Restoration and validation of Entra Connect (Azure AD Connect) synchronization infrastructure to ensure on-premises-to-cloud identity flows are accurate, authorized, and free of attacker-introduced misconfigurations or backdoors.
- **OAuth application cleanup**
  - Identification and removal of attacker-registered or overprivileged OAuth applications, consent grants, and delegated permissions that enable persistent access to Microsoft 365, Entra ID, or third-party cloud resources.
- **Refresh Token revocation**
  - Invalidation of long-lived refresh tokens issued to applications and users to terminate persistent authenticated sessions and force re-authentication across all client endpoints.
- **Session invalidation**
  - Forced termination of active authentication sessions across web, mobile, and thick-client applications to immediately sever adversary access and require identity re-verification.
- **Conditional Access recovery validation**
  - Post-incident verification that Conditional Access policies correctly enforce intended security controls, have not been bypassed or disabled by attackers, and are restored to pre-incident or hardened configurations.
- **Hybrid identity recovery**
  - Coordinated restoration of synchronization, federation, and authentication pathways between on-premises Active Directory and cloud identity platforms to ensure integrity and eliminate attacker-established persistence.
- **Identity verification and validation**
  - Confirmation that user, device, and service identities are authentic, properly provisioned, and operating within authorized parameters during and after incident response activities.
- **Identity reverification and revalidation**
  - Mandatory re-confirmation of identity attributes, credentials, and trustworthiness for users, administrators, and devices after a security incident to ensure no compromised or synthetic identities persist in the environment.
- **Post-incident hardening**
  - Application of additional security controls, configuration changes, and architectural improvements to identity infrastructure following an incident to reduce recurrence likelihood and attack surface.
- **Recovery validation**
  - Systematic verification that all restored identity systems, policies, and trust relationships function correctly and securely before returning to normal operations.
- **Return-to-Operations (RTO) criteria**
  - Defined benchmarks - including threat eradication confirmation, control validation, stakeholder approval, and monitoring readiness - that must be met before declaring identity infrastructure safe for resumed production use

This module frames recovery as a trust restoration function. Readers learn that defenders must prove the identity infrastructure is trustworthy again, not merely restore services quickly.

Operational lessons learned are deferred to Module 15. Future modernization is deferred to Module 16.

**Federal / DoD Operational Perspective**

Module 14 examines identity recovery and domain trust restoration as mission-critical recovery operations across federal civilian agencies, Defense Agencies, Combatant Commands, Intelligence Community elements, military departments, and mission-partner environments.

The operational thesis is that after identity compromise, the central question is not "Are the systems back online?" The central question is "Can the mission trust the identity infrastructure again?" When adversaries compromise Domain Controllers, perform DCSync, expose NTDS.dit, forge Kerberos tickets, manipulate AD CS, abuse federation, compromise service accounts, or establish Tier 0 persistence, ordinary restoration is insufficient. The agency or Command must validate that authentication, authorization, credential issuance, certificate trust, federation trust, privileged access, and administrative control are reliable.

In federal and defense environments, recovery may affect mission systems, operational networks, administrative enclaves, cloud services, certificate authorities, federation partners, classified and unclassified resources, coalition access, contractor-managed services, remote access, and continuity of operations. Recovery decisions must account for operational tempo, evidence preservation, authorization boundaries, mission impact, commander intent, privacy requirements, reporting obligations, CSSP visibility, and authorizing official risk decisions.

This module frames recovery as a mission assurance function because commanders, system owners, ISSOs, ISSMs, authorizing officials, security engineers, identity administrators, SOC analysts, CSSPs, incident responders, mission owners, and mission partners need evidence-based confidence that the identity control plane has been restored to trusted operation.

**Transition**

Having established how defenders recover identity infrastructure and restore domain trust after compromise, the reader is now prepared to examine why identity failures happen in real operational environments even when policies, tools, and procedures exist on paper. Module 15 transitions from recovery into operational lessons learned by examining forgotten privileged accounts, shared administrative passwords, BloodHound findings nobody fixed, documentation drift, temporary service accounts that became permanent, broken PKI implementations, excessive delegation, poor RMF implementation, STIGs checked off but never actually implemented, contractor turnover, mission pressure, politics versus security, ISSO frustrations, and the operational realities that rarely appear in official documentation.
